Approximation & Par debt at \(r_c < r\) & Subsidy about 23\% of \(I\) at
\(\ell = 0.40\); a fairly priced coupon is 2.5 times the par coupon;
raises \(X_L\) by 37\% at \(\ell = 0.40\), where fairly priced debt
lowers it by 0.4\% \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Unconditional-\(A_{\text{eff}}\) discounting.} The pure-power
approximation of Section~\ref{sec-single-firm} values every option-like
L-regime payoff with the H-regime exponent \(\beta_H\) and the
unconditional coefficient \(A_{\text{eff}}\) (Equation~\ref{eq-a-eff})
in place of regime-contingent value functions. Applied to the duopoly it
gives a reduced-form preemption game, in which the follower's option is
proportional to \(X^{\beta_H}\), the leader's monopoly-phase value
discounts follower entry by \((X/X_F^*)^{\beta_H}\), and the leader is
held at the single firm's pure-power scale. That game is no longer the
paper's benchmark. It is the nested approximation against which
Table~\ref{tbl-domains} measures the exact equilibrium, and it remains
in use in Section~\ref{sec-calibration} and Section~\ref{sec-valuation},
where the symbols \(X^*\) and \(K^*\) denote the pure-power policy
\((X^{\text{pp}}, K^{\text{pp}})\) of Equation~\ref{eq-trigger-phi} and
not the exact policy of Proposition 1(iii). In
Section~\ref{sec-duopoly}, by contrast, \(X_L\), \(X_P\), \(K_L\),
\(\phi_L\), \(X_F^*\) and \(K_F\) denote objects of the exact game, and
\(K_L\), \(\phi_L\), \(X_F^*\) and \(K_F\) are evaluated for a leader
that enters at \(X_L\) unless stated otherwise. The approximation is not
a small signed error around the exact problem: its triggers and
capacities are objects of the model it defines, while \(\phi^*\) carries
over exactly to the single firm, because it maximizes \(A_{\text{eff}}\)
at every scale.

\textbf{Leader's scale in the duopoly.} The leader chooses its capacity
and training fraction at the demand at which it enters, with the
follower best-responding, so \((K_L, \phi_L)\) is a best response in the
game and not the single firm's policy. The choice matters for existence
and not only for levels. With the leader held at the single firm's
pure-power policy \((K^{\text{pp}}, \phi^*)\), leading is never better
than following at baseline (the leader's value stays at least 5.4\%
below the follower's) and no rent-equalization trigger exists. With the
leader held at the single firm's precommitted-scale policy such a
trigger exists, but only barely, and leading is then worth less than
mutual waiting (Internet Appendix B).

\textbf{Single-boundary default.} The default boundary
Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary} replaces the H-regime equity value by
its perpetuity component, collapsing the coupled pair \((X_D^L, X_D^H)\)
into one boundary. This is an approximation whose bias is signed and
quantified in Table~\ref{tbl-domains}: it overstates \(X_D\) and so errs
against faith-based survival. The same reduction gives the unlevered
firm's shutdown boundary, which is Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary} at
a zero coupon. Proposition 2 uses it. The entry equilibrium and the
credit exhibits of Section~\ref{sec-valuation} do not: each claim there
carries the boundary of the coupled problem (Internet Appendix B).

\textbf{Par debt issuance at an exogenous coupon.} Leverage \(\ell\) and
the coupon rate \(r_c\) are exogenous, and debt is issued at par: the
firm receives \(\ell I(K)\) against a perpetual coupon
\(r_c \ell I(K)\). Because \(r_c < r\), the convention embeds an
implicit financing subsidy of \(\ell(1 - r_c/r) I(K)\)
(Table~\ref{tbl-domains}). It is a stress-test device that varies the
debt burden without solving a capital-structure problem, and it is why
the leverage comparative statics are not endogenous capital-structure
results. The fairly priced alternative sets the coupon so that the debt
is worth its proceeds \(\ell I(K)\) at issuance; it requires a coupon
rate above \(r\) on those proceeds, \(12.3\%\) at \(\ell = 0.40\) for
debt issued at the single-firm trigger, about two and a half times the
par coupon (Internet Appendix A). The entry equilibrium is solved under
both conventions, and Remark 4 reports which effects of leverage are
artifacts of the subsidy.

Proposition 1 and the equilibrium of Proposition 3 are statements about
the exact problem, the second a numerical one; Proposition 2 is an exact
statement about the single-boundary reduction.

\subsection{Duopoly with Default Risk}\label{sec-duopoly}

I now introduce strategic interaction and leverage. Two symmetric firms
each hold options to invest in capacity and choose their
training-inference allocation.

Because the contest shares below depend on own capacity, the
separability behind the closed-form capacity of Proposition 1 fails in
the duopoly. Both firms' policies are obtained numerically, and the
leader's capacity and training fraction are chosen at the demand at
which it enters (Section~\ref{sec-conventions}).

\subsubsection{Regime-specific
competition}\label{regime-specific-competition}

Revenue in each regime is governed by a Tullock contest success function
\citep{tullock1980efficient, skaperdas1996contest} over the
regime-relevant capacity measure, so revenue depends on \emph{relative}
capacity. In the L-regime, firms compete over \emph{inference capacity}:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-contest-L}{
\pi_i^L(X, \mathbf{K}, \boldsymbol{\phi}) = X \cdot \frac{[(1-\phi_i)K_i]^{2\alpha}}{[(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha + [(1-\phi_j)K_j]^\alpha},
}\end{equation} which I write equivalently as
\(\pi_i^L = X \cdot [(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha \cdot s_i^L\), where
\(s_i^L = [(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha / \{[(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha + [(1-\phi_j)K_j]^\alpha\}\)
is the L-regime contest share. In the H-regime, firms compete over
\emph{training compute}:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-contest-H}{
\pi_i^H(X, \mathbf{K}, \boldsymbol{\phi}) = X \cdot \frac{(\phi_i K_i)^{2\alpha}}{(\phi_i K_i)^\alpha + (\phi_j K_j)^\alpha},
}\end{equation} with H-regime contest share
\(s_i^H = (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha / \{(\phi_i K_i)^\alpha + (\phi_j K_j)^\alpha\}\).

The specification formalizes the arms-race logic executives describe:
today what matters is the capacity to \emph{serve} demand, in a post-AGI
world the capability of a firm's \emph{models}. When \(K_j = 0\) both
shares equal one and revenue collapses to the single-firm case; under
symmetric capacities each share is \(1/2\). Under asymmetry two objects
must be kept apart: the contest share \(s_i = z^\alpha/(1+z^\alpha)\),
with \(z\) the ratio of own to rival regime-relevant capacity, is
increasing and concave in own capacity for \(\alpha < 1\), while the
revenue share \(z^{2\alpha}/(1+z^{2\alpha})\) exceeds it but, for
\(\alpha < 1/2\), still stays below the physical capacity fraction. The
specification rewards scale less than proportionally rather than
producing a winner-take-all pattern, and what asymmetry adds is revenue
expansion (Internet Appendix E bounds its contribution with the
arithmetic-mean industry-revenue benchmark).

Revenue expansion means that the leader's gain from capacity dominance
partly reflects pie expansion, not just share theft, which could amplify
preemption incentives. Internet Appendix E re-solves the equilibrium
under an arithmetic-mean industry-revenue benchmark, under which the
training fraction and the survival threshold are unchanged and only the
preemption discount moves, and discusses why the mechanisms carry over
to Cournot competition.

The training fraction enters both contests and moves a firm's position
in opposite directions across regimes: a higher \(\phi_i\) raises the
firm's H-regime share and lowers its L-regime share. Proposition 3(ii)
states when this trade-off leaves the allocation at \(\phi^*\) and by
how much the leader departs from it.

\subsubsection{Installed value in the
duopoly}\label{installed-value-in-the-duopoly}

The installed value for firm \(i\) in the L-regime combines inference
revenue and the expected H-regime continuation:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-duopoly-value-L}{
V_i^L(X) = A_{\text{eff},i} \cdot X - \frac{\delta K_i}{r},
}\end{equation} where the effective revenue coefficient for firm \(i\)
is: \begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-a-eff-duopoly}{
A_{\text{eff},i} = \frac{[(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha \cdot s_i^L}{r - \mu_L + \lambda} + \frac{\lambda}{r - \mu_L + \lambda} \cdot \frac{(\phi_i K_i)^\alpha \cdot s_i^H}{r - \mu_H}.
}\end{equation}

The H-regime value is
\(V_i^H(X) = A_H X (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha s_i^H - \delta K_i/r\). The two
terms of Equation~\ref{eq-a-eff-duopoly} carry the central trade-off,
inference revenue now against expected training revenue later, each
scaled by the corresponding contest share.

\subsubsection{Capital structure}\label{capital-structure}

Capital structure is parameterized by the leverage ratio
\(\ell = P / I(K)\), with \(P\) the face value of debt, exogenous and
common to both firms; debt is issued at par and pays a continuous coupon
\(C_D = r_c \cdot \ell \cdot I(K)\). Leverage is a \emph{stress-test
parameter}, not an equilibrium financing choice, and par issuance embeds
an implicit financing subsidy (Table~\ref{tbl-domains}), so no leverage
result below is an endogenous capital-structure result. At \(\ell = 0\)
the coupon is zero and the formulas below describe the unlevered firm.

\subsubsection{Default boundary}\label{default-boundary}

Following \citet{leland1994corporate} and \citet{leland1996optimal},
equity holders stop funding the firm when equity value reaches zero, and
the optimal boundary follows from smooth pasting. For a firm with
\((K_i, \phi_i)\) facing a rival with \((K_j, \phi_j)\):
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-default-boundary}{
X_D = \frac{\beta_L^-}{\beta_L^- - 1} \cdot \frac{C_D / r + \delta K_i / r}{A_{\text{eff},i}},
}\end{equation} where \(\beta_L^-\) is the negative root of the L-regime
characteristic equation, which incorporates the regime switch through
the effective discount rate \(r + \lambda\), and
\(\beta_L^-/(\beta_L^- - 1) \in (0,1)\) is the option value of waiting
before defaulting.\footnote{Replacing the H-regime equity value by its
  perpetuity component yields Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary}
  exactly; Table~\ref{tbl-domains} reports the size and sign of the
  bias.}

The boundary has two fixed claims in its numerator, the coupon and the
operating cost, and the second does not depend on debt. With debt,
\(X_D\) is a \emph{default boundary}. Without debt (\(C_D = 0\)) the
same expression is the \emph{shutdown boundary} at which an all-equity
owner stops operating, so \(X_D\) is continuous in leverage at
\(\ell = 0\) and leverage shifts a boundary that exists without it. The
shift is small at baseline: at \(\ell = 0.40\) under par debt the
operating-cost claim \(\delta K/r\) is about twelve times the coupon
claim \(C_D/r\) at the single firm's precommitted-scale capacity and
twenty-three times at the leader's, so that leverage raises the boundary
by \(8.4\%\) and \(4.4\%\) above the shutdown boundary.

\(A_{\text{eff},i}\) includes the H-regime continuation value through
the \(\lambda\) term. This is the \emph{training-survival channel}, the
route by which allocation feeds back into solvency. Because
\(A_{\text{eff},i}\) is single-peaked in \(\phi_i\) and the boundary is
inversely proportional to it, \(X_D\) is U-shaped in the training
fraction, falling up to \(\phi^*\) and rising beyond it. I call the
default-boundary implication of optimism \emph{faith-based survival}: a
higher \(\lambda\) raises the weight on the H-regime continuation value
and, when the firm trains enough for that term to matter, lowers the
boundary.

The mechanism differs from models in which the growth option is
exogenous, where a larger option unambiguously lowers the default
boundary: here the growth option is controlled by \(\phi\), and raising
\(\phi\) also cuts current inference revenue. The thresholds below are
thresholds on the effect of \emph{beliefs} at a given \(\phi_i\), not on
the effect of \(\phi_i\) itself, which at a given belief is the U-shape
just described.\footnote{The \(A_{\text{eff}}\)-channel threshold
  \(\underline{\phi}\) is independent of \(\lambda\); the exact net
  threshold \(\tilde{\phi}\), which absorbs the markup channel operating
  through the discount root, depends on it.}

\textbf{Proposition 2} (Endogenous default with faith-based survival;
duopoly). \emph{For a firm in the duopoly with leverage \(\ell \geq 0\),
fixed capacity \((K_i, \phi_i)\) and rival \((K_j, \phi_j)\), the
boundary \(X_D\) of Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary}, a default
boundary when \(\ell > 0\) and a shutdown boundary when \(\ell = 0\),
has the following properties:}

\emph{(i) \(X_D\) is increasing in leverage \(\ell\) and, when
\(\ell > 0\), in the coupon rate \(r_c\).}

\emph{(ii) \(X_D\) is decreasing in \(\lambda\) precisely when the
training allocation is sufficiently high, and the characterization is
fully closed-form in the symmetric-shares case (\(s_i^L = s_i^H\), as
under symmetric capacities} and \emph{allocations, or monopoly). Two
channels operate. The \(A_{\text{eff}}\) channel (higher \(\lambda\)
raises the weight on the H-regime continuation value, lowering \(X_D\))
is positive if and only if \(\phi_i\) exceeds}
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-phi-underbar}{
\underline{\phi} = \frac{\Omega}{1 + \Omega}, \quad \Omega \equiv \left(\frac{r - \mu_H}{r - \mu_L}\right)^{1/\alpha}.
}\end{equation} \emph{An opposing markup channel operates through the
discount root: higher \(\lambda\) raises the effective discount rate and
pushes \(\beta_L^-/(\beta_L^- - 1)\) toward one, raising \(X_D\).
Because the \(A_{\text{eff}}\)-channel semi-elasticity is linear in the
L-regime flow and H-regime continuation terms, the net condition
\(\partial X_D / \partial \lambda < 0\) also solves in closed form: it
holds if and only if \(\phi_i\) exceeds the exact threshold}
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-phi-tilde}{
\tilde{\phi} = \frac{\tilde{\Omega}}{1 + \tilde{\Omega}}, \quad \tilde{\Omega} \equiv \left[\frac{(r - \mu_H)\,(1 + m\Delta)}{(r - \mu_L) - m\lambda\Delta}\right]^{1/\alpha},
}\end{equation} \emph{where \(\Delta = r - \mu_L + \lambda\) and
\(m = \mathrm{d}\ln M / \mathrm{d}\lambda = 1/[\beta_L^-(\beta_L^- - 1)\mathcal{R}] > 0\)
is the markup-channel semi-elasticity, with
\(\mathcal{R} = \sqrt{(\mu_L - \sigma^2/2)^2 + 2\sigma^2(r+\lambda)}\)
(Internet Appendix A states the mild regularity condition). The exact
threshold nests \(\underline{\phi}\) as the \(m \to 0\) limit and
satisfies \(\tilde{\phi} > \underline{\phi}\). Unlike
\(\underline{\phi}\), \(\tilde{\phi}\) depends on \(\lambda\). Finally,
\(\underline{\phi}\) is increasing in \(\alpha\) (with less concavity in
the revenue function, a larger training allocation is needed for the
H-regime term to dominate), decreasing in \(\mu_H - \mu_L\) (a larger
regime-switch growth premium amplifies the H-regime term, lowering the
threshold), and increasing in \(r\) (higher discounting reduces the
present value of the continuation opportunity).}

\emph{(iii) For a given capacity \(K\) and training fraction \(\phi_i\)
below the interior maximizer of \(A_{\text{eff},i}(\cdot)\), there
exists a leverage-training substitution along the iso-\(X_D\) locus: a
firm can maintain the same default boundary \(X_D\) while increasing
leverage (which raises the coupon obligation in the numerator) by
simultaneously increasing the training fraction (which raises
\(A_{\text{eff},i}\) through the H-regime term in the denominator). This
is a mechanical relationship describing the set of \((\ell, \phi)\)
pairs consistent with a given default boundary, not a statement about
optimality: in the solved model the optimal training fraction is
invariant to leverage, since the allocation first-order condition does
not involve \(\ell\).}

\emph{(iv) \(X_D\) is increasing in rival capacity \(K_j\), because
higher rival capacity reduces the contest shares \(s_i^L\) and
\(s_i^H\).}

Parts (i)--(iv) hold at fixed \((K, \phi, \ell)\), a domain
Table~\ref{tbl-domains} records; part (i) is a stress-test comparative
static rather than a statement about an optimally chosen capital
structure, as are Remark 4 and Section~\ref{sec-valuation}. Under joint
re-optimization, higher \(\lambda\) also raises \(\phi^*\) (Proposition
1), but because \(\phi^*\) maximizes \(A_{\text{eff}}\), this induced
reallocation has no first-order effect on \(X_D\) (an envelope argument;
at baseline about \(4\%\) of the direct effect of moving \(\lambda\)
from \(0.10\) to \(0.11\)). Three experiments should be kept apart.
Raising \(\lambda\) at a fixed policy lowers \(X_D\) if and only if
\(\phi_i > \tilde{\phi}\) (part ii). Raising \(\phi_i\) at a fixed
belief lowers \(X_D\) if and only if \(\phi_i < \phi^*\), and raises it
beyond. Comparing firms that re-optimize under different beliefs
combines the two: along the locus of optimal policies
\(X_D(\phi^*(\lambda), \lambda)\) is decreasing in \(\lambda\) for
\(\lambda \gtrsim 0.034\) at baseline, so a more optimistic firm trains
more \emph{and} sits further from default, but it is the belief, not the
training fraction, that does the work. Because the optimal training
fraction is invariant to leverage, part (iii) carries an empirical
caveat: observed comovement between leverage and training allocation
would reflect joint determination rather than a causal link.

At baseline, \(\Omega \approx 0.22\), \(\underline{\phi} \approx 0.18\),
and \(\tilde{\phi} \approx 0.32\) against \(\phi^* \approx 0.70\), so
faith-based survival holds at the optimum. Because \(\tilde{\phi}\)
depends on \(\lambda\), the ranking reverses for
\(\lambda \lesssim 0.034\), where the optimal allocation is too
inference-heavy for the training-survival channel to dominate.

Figure~\ref{fig-default-boundaries} shows how debt moves both firms'
entry triggers and boundaries in the equilibrium of the exact game, at
the leverage levels of Table~\ref{tbl-duopoly-leverage}. Under par debt
every line rises with leverage: from \(\ell = 0\) to \(0.65\) the first
investment moves from \(0.0034\) to \(0.0059\) and the follower's
trigger from \(0.044\) to \(0.078\), while the leader's distance to its
boundary at entry falls from \(4.8\) to \(4.3\) and the follower's from
\(10.3\) to \(6.0\). Under fairly priced debt the triggers barely move
and the boundaries still rise, to distances of \(4.1\) and \(5.0\)
(Remark 4). The band that bears on default along the equilibrium path is
the leader's: from \(X_L\) the follower's trigger is reached before the
switch with probability \(0.4\%\), and the follower enters \(107\) years
after the leader in expectation.

\begin{figure}[!htb]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_default_boundaries.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-default-boundaries}Entry triggers and boundaries of
the two firms against leverage in the equilibrium of the exact game. (a)
Par debt at coupon rate \(r_c = 0.05\). (b) Fairly priced debt. Blue
squares: first investment \(X_L\) (solid) and the leader's boundary
\(X_{D,L}\) at its entry (dash-dotted); black circles: the follower's
post-switch trigger \(X_F^*\) (solid) and its boundary \(X_{D,F}\) at
its entry (dashed). Each shaded band runs from a firm's boundary to its
entry trigger. The equilibrium is solved at each leverage level
\(\ell \in \{0, 0.20, 0.40, 0.65\}\) (Table~\ref{tbl-duopoly-leverage}),
with the leader's capacity and training fraction chosen at its entry
demand and the follower's policy re-optimized; the boundaries are those
of the coupled regime-switching claims, and at \(\ell = 0\) the shutdown
boundaries. The vertical axis is logarithmic. Parameters: baseline
calibration, bankruptcy cost \(b = 0.30\), recovery rule
Equation~\ref{eq-recovery}.}

\end{figure}%

\subsubsection{Equity and debt values}\label{equity-and-debt-values}

Following \citet{leland1994corporate}, \(E(X)\) is the
\emph{going-concern} equity value once investment is sunk, and the
object relevant for timing is the entry NPV \(E(X) - (1-\ell)I(K)\).
This is the single equity convention used in the paper, the proofs, and
the code: limited liability floors \(E\), not the entry NPV. The equity
contribution is sunk and is not refunded on default, so the entry NPV is
strictly negative at low demand, the boundary condition on which
Proposition 3(i) rests. Above the default boundary:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-equity}{
E(X) = A_{\text{eff},i}\, X - \frac{\delta K}{r} - \frac{C_D}{r} + \left[\frac{C_D}{r} + \frac{\delta K}{r} - A_{\text{eff},i}\, X_D \right]\left(\frac{X}{X_D}\right)^{\beta_L^-},
}\end{equation} where \(\beta_L^- < 0\). The last term is the default
option and the bracket the flow deficit at the boundary; value-matching
and smooth-pasting on \(E\) pin down \(X_D\) in
Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary}. At \(X = X_D\), \(E(X_D) = 0\) and
the entry NPV equals \(-(1-\ell)I(K) < 0\), as below \(X_D\). Without
debt, Equation~\ref{eq-equity} at \(C_D = 0\) is the \emph{unlevered
asset value} \(U(X)\), the installed value plus the option to shut down
at the shutdown boundary, so equity is continuous in leverage at
\(\ell = 0\).\footnote{The shutdown option is \(0.25\%\) of the entry
  NPV at the pure-power trigger, where demand is 5.3 times the shutdown
  boundary, and \(0.14\%\) at the precommitted-scale policy of
  Proposition 1(iii). In the entry game, whose leader enters at \(4.8\)
  times its shutdown boundary, including it lowers the rent-equalization
  trigger by \(4.3\%\).}

Debt value is: \[
D(X) = \frac{C_D}{r}\left[1 - \left(\frac{X}{X_D}\right)^{\beta_L^-}\right] + R(X_D) \left(\frac{X}{X_D}\right)^{\beta_L^-},
\] where the recovery in default is
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-recovery}{
R(X_D) = \min\left\{(1 - b)\,\min\left\{\Lambda(X_D),\; U(X_D)\right\},\; \frac{C_D}{r}\right\},
}\end{equation} where \[
\Lambda(X) = \begin{cases} a_i X - c_i + (c_i - a_i X_I)\left(\dfrac{X}{X_I}\right)^{\beta_L^-} & X > X_I, \\ 0 & X \leq X_I, \end{cases}
\] with \(a_i = [(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha s_i^L/(r - \mu_L + \lambda)\) the
inference revenue coefficient, \(c_i = \delta(1-\phi_i)K_i/r\) the
operating cost of the inference capacity, and
\(X_I = [\beta_L^-/(\beta_L^- - 1)]\, c_i/a_i\). Here \(b \in [0,1]\) is
the fraction of liquidation value lost in bankruptcy and
\(\Lambda(X_D)\) the liquidation value of productive assets. Only the
\emph{inference} business survives liquidation, while the H-regime
continuation value is destroyed, because the post-AGI payoff requires
the going concern. The liquidation value is that of the inference
business to an owner who carries the same perpetual cost \(\delta\) per
unit of capacity as the firm, which is the cost that brings the firm to
\(X_D\) in the first place, and who, like the unlevered firm, may shut
the business down: \(X_I\) is Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary} applied
to the inference business alone at a zero coupon, and \(\Lambda\) is
that business's unlevered asset value in the same single-boundary form
as \(U\). As in \citet{leland1994corporate}, what creditors take over is
an asset value. Without the option term, \(a_i X - c_i\) plus the
training business is exactly the installed value
Equation~\ref{eq-installed-value-L}. Recovery is capped twice: by the
unlevered asset value \(U(X_D)\) of the assets seized, because a small
inference capacity can be worth more than a whole firm that cannot shed
its training capacity, and by the default-free value of the claim.

Faith is worthless to creditors \emph{in} default, but that does not
make a higher training fraction raise the loss given default where the
model's firms operate. Revenue is concave in capacity and the operating
cost linear, so moving capacity into training raises inference revenue
per unit of inference capacity and lowers \(X_I\). At \(\phi^*\) the two
businesses earn the same revenue per unit of capacity, so \(X_I\) equals
the firm's shutdown boundary and \(\Lambda\) is the fraction
\(1 - \phi^*\) of \(U\); with less training \(X_I\) lies above the
shutdown boundary, and recovery is zero until the coupon raises \(X_D\)
above it. Recovery is therefore weakly increasing in \(\phi\) around the
model's policies, and small there: at most \(5\%\) of the default-free
claim at the single firm's and the leader's entry for
\(\ell \leq 0.70\), so debt is risky from the first unit and the loss
given default is \(95\%\) or more (Section~\ref{sec-valuation}). A
liquidator who could not shut the inference business down would recover
nothing at those policies at any leverage, because at \(X_D\) total
revenue covers only \(\beta_L^-/(\beta_L^- - 1) \approx 0.70\) of the
fixed claims (Internet Appendix A).

\subsubsection{Entry equilibrium}\label{entry-equilibrium}

I solve the timing game in the primitives of Section~\ref{sec-model},
without the pure-power form. Demand is in regime \(L\) until the switch
and in regime \(H\) after it, either firm may invest before or after the
switch, and every claim held before the switch solves the L-regime
equation Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L} with its own flow, and its own value at
the switch, as forcing (Internet Appendix B). Investment is publicly
observed. One firm, the leader, installs first and the other
best-responds, as in the timing game of \citet{fudenberg1985preemption},
extended to capacity choice by \citet{huisman2015strategic} and
\citet{pawlina2006real}. Two demand levels organize entry: the trigger
\(X_L\) that a firm chooses when it knows that its rival will follow,
and the rent-equalization trigger \(X_P\) of the preemption
construction, the lowest demand level at which leading is no worse than
following. The benchmark is the equilibrium in which one firm leads at
\(X_L\) and the other follows, and \(X_P\) is an upper bound on the date
of first investment. Neither trigger has a closed form. I verify the
no-deviation conditions of the benchmark numerically and do not
characterize the full set of equilibria of the timing game; Proposition
3 and Table~\ref{tbl-domains} state what is established, and on what
grid.

\textbf{Follower's problem.} The follower observes \((K_L, \phi_L)\). If
the switch arrives before it has invested, it holds an option in the
absorbing regime against an installed rival: it devotes all its capacity
to training, since inference earns nothing after the switch, and enters
at a trigger \(X_F^*\) with capacity \(K_F\). If the switch finds demand
already above \(X_F^*\), it enters at once, at the capacity that is
optimal at that demand. Before the switch it may also enter, choosing
\((K, \phi)\) against the leader in both contests, and it does so only
where entry is worth more than waiting for the switch. Its value
\(F(X)\) is the value of that stopping problem.

\textbf{Leader's problem.} A leader that enters at demand \(X\) chooses
\((K_L, \phi_L)\) to maximize its entry value
\(L(X; K_L, \phi_L) = E_L(X) - (1-\ell)I(K_L)\), anticipating the
follower's response. It earns monopoly inference revenue until the
follower enters, monopoly training revenue after the switch until demand
reaches the follower's trigger, and shared revenue afterwards. I write
\(L^*(X) = \max_{K, \phi} L(X; K, \phi)\). The leader's scale is
therefore a best response at the entry demand and not the single firm's
scale, a difference that decides whether a trigger exists
(Section~\ref{sec-conventions}).

\textbf{First investment and rent equalization.} Both firms share the
same \(\lambda\) and the game is symmetric; cross-sectional variation in
investment behavior (Section~\ref{sec-calibration},
Section~\ref{sec-valuation}) comes from comparative statics across
archetypes, not from interaction among firms with different priors. What
a firm is worth before either has invested depends on what happens if
the switch arrives first. Let \(W(X)\) be the value to each firm when
neither invests before the switch. At the switch the two firms play the
entry game of the absorbing regime, which is a preemption race, so that
each is worth the follower's value in it (Internet Appendix B). A firm
that leads at a trigger \(\bar{X}\), its rival never investing first, is
worth \(W(X) + [L^*(\bar{X}) - W(\bar{X})](X/\bar{X})^{\beta_L^+}\)
below it, and \(X_L\) is the trigger that maximizes this value:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-first-investment}{
X_L = \arg\max_{X}\; \bigl[L^*(X) - W(X)\bigr]\,X^{-\beta_L^+}.
}\end{equation} The rent-equalization trigger \(X_P\) is the lowest
demand level at which \(L^*(X) = F(X)\), with \(F\) the follower's value
against the policy the leader chooses at \(X\). Above it each firm would
rather lead than follow, so the first investment cannot come later. In
the standard preemption game \(X_P < X_L\): leading is the better role
at the leader's own trigger, the two firms race, and entry is brought
forward to \(X_P\), where the rent from leading is dissipated. When
instead \(X_L < X_P\), following is the better role at \(X_L\), and a
firm gains nothing by investing ahead of a rival that will lead there.
The strategies in which one firm leads at \(X_L\) and the other follows
then form an equilibrium if two conditions hold. The leader must prefer
\(X_L\) to every other stopping rule, given that its rival never invests
first. The follower must prefer waiting to investing first at every
demand level below \(X_L\), where its value is that of waiting for the
leader's entry or for the switch,
\(W(X) + [F(X_L) - W(X_L)](X/X_L)^{\beta_L^+}\), and not \(F(X)\). That
value exceeds the leader's whenever \(F(X_L) \geq L^*(X_L)\), so the
second condition follows from the first whenever \(X_L \leq X_P\).
Proposition 3(iii) reports that this is the case on the whole grid.

\textbf{Default, entry, and what the equilibrium does not cover.} Each
installed firm may stop: equity holders abandon the firm at a boundary,
a shutdown boundary without debt and a default boundary with it,
creditors recover \(R(X_D)\), and only the inference business is
realized in liquidation. In the game that boundary is the one of the
coupled regime-switching problem, in which the claim held after the
switch has its own boundary; it is not
Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary}, which overstates it
(Table~\ref{tbl-domains}). What happens once a firm stops lies outside
the equilibrium characterized here. The recovery formula values the
liquidated inference assets but does not say who operates them, so it
determines neither the survivor's revenue nor the opportunity of a
follower that has not yet entered when the leader stops. Each firm
therefore values its claim as if the rival's capacity stayed in the
contest, and all quantities below are computed on the path along which
both firms keep operating.

\textbf{Proposition 3} (Entry in the duopoly with the leader's scale
chosen at entry; symmetric duopoly). \emph{In the symmetric duopoly with
leverage \(\ell \geq 0\), in the exact primitives:}

\emph{(i) (Analytical; the follower after the switch, for the claim
without the option to stop) Against a leader with training capacity
\(T_L = \phi_L K_L\), a follower that has not invested when the switch
arrives devotes all capacity to training, installs the capacity \(K_F\)
that solves}
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-follower-foc}{
\alpha \beta_H \bigl(2 - s_F(K)\bigr)\,\Theta(K) = (\beta_H - 1)\,K\,\Theta'(K), \qquad s_F(K) = \frac{K^\alpha}{K^\alpha + T_L^\alpha}, \quad \Theta(K) = \frac{\delta K}{r} + I(K),
}\end{equation} \emph{and enters at
\(X_F^* = [\beta_H/(\beta_H - 1)]\,\Theta(K_F)/[A_H K_F^\alpha s_F(K_F)]\).
The solution is unique whenever \((\beta_H - 1)/(2\alpha\beta_H) < 1\)
and \((\beta_H - 1)/(\alpha\beta_H) > 1/\gamma\), the second of which is
the lower bound of (A2). The follower's marginal revenue elasticity is
\(\alpha(2 - s_F)\) where the single firm's is \(\alpha\), and at
baseline the follower builds far more than the single firm and enters
later. With the option to stop, \(K_F\) and \(X_F^*\) are computed
numerically and differ from these by less than \(0.2\%\) at baseline.}

\emph{(ii) (Analytical critical point; equilibrium allocation numerical)
For a firm that enters before the switch against an installed rival with
the same training fraction \(\phi\), the contest shares coincide across
the two regimes and the allocation first-order condition is that of the
single firm, whatever the two capacities: a common allocation is a
critical point of its allocation problem if and only if it is \(\phi^*\)
of Proposition 1. In equilibrium the leader is not at \(\phi^*\). It
chooses \(\phi_L < \phi^*\), by \(3.1\%\) at baseline and by \(14\%\) at
\(\lambda = 0.03\), the gap closing as \(\lambda\) rises (\(0.2\%\) at
\(\lambda = 0.50\)), because its post-switch training revenue is diluted
by a follower that trains with all its capacity while its pre-switch
inference revenue is a monopoly. Role invariance of the training
fraction is thus approximate for the leader and does not apply to a
follower that enters after the switch.}

\emph{(iii) (Numerical finding; an equilibrium verified on a demand
scan, no uniqueness claim) At baseline and at every arrival rate on the
grid
\(\lambda \in \{0.03, 0.05, 0.075, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30, 0.40, 0.50\}\),
the trigger \(X_L\) of Equation~\ref{eq-first-investment} is finite and
lies below the rent-equalization trigger \(X_P\), which also exists, and
the strategies in which one firm invests first at \(X_L\) and the other
follows satisfy the no-deviation conditions on the scanned range of
demand, from \(0.03\) to \(10\) times \(X_P\): the leader's stopping
problem, solved without restricting it to a trigger, has \(X_L\) as the
lower end of its investment region; the follower's value of waiting
exceeds the value of investing first at every demand level up to
\(X_L\), by at least \(6.8\%\) at baseline and \(0.4\%\) at
\(\lambda = 0.50\); and the follower's entry policy after the leader's
is its best response. The arrival rates include those at which the
single firm that chooses its scale freely never invests before the
switch (\(\lambda \geq 0.073\)). At baseline and zero leverage,
\(X_L = 0.0034\), \(K_L = 0.0042\) and \(\phi_L = 0.679\); the follower
waits for the switch at every demand level and then enters at
\(X_F^* = 0.044\) with \(K_F = 0.16\); leading at \(X_L\) is worth
\(1.09\) times the value of mutual waiting, and following is worth
\(1.10\) times leading. The rent-equalization trigger is
\(X_P = 0.0086\), and \(X_L/X_P\) is \(0.31\) at \(\lambda = 0.03\),
\(0.39\) at baseline and \(0.97\) at \(\lambda = 0.50\). Between \(X_L\)
and \(X_P\) following is the better role, yet a firm prefers investing
to waiting for \(X_P\): a second-mover advantage, not a race to be
first. This equilibrium and its mirror image, in which the roles are
exchanged, are the ones characterized. Whether other asymmetric
equilibria exist is not established, and the symmetric equilibrium in
mixed strategies, which would involve randomized delay between \(X_L\)
and \(X_P\), is not solved.}

\emph{(iv) (Numerical finding) The trigger and the leader's capacity are
hump-shaped in the arrival rate, with a maximum near \(\lambda = 0.15\)
on the grid: \(X_L\) rises from \(0.000042\) at \(\lambda = 0.03\) to
\(0.0034\) at \(0.10\) and \(0.0038\) at \(0.15\) and falls to
\(0.0014\) at \(0.50\), and \(K_L\) from \(0.0000043\) to \(0.0042\),
\(0.0056\) and \(0.0020\). More optimistic leaders invest earlier only
above \(\lambda \approx 0.15\), and the leader's capacity is not
invariant to beliefs.}

\emph{(v) (Numerical finding) The leader's distance to its boundary at
entry, \(X_L/X_D\), is \(4.8\) at baseline without debt. It is
hump-shaped in \(\lambda\) with a maximum of \(4.9\) near \(0.15\) and
falls to \(4.1\) at \(\lambda = 0.50\), so that above
\(\lambda \approx 0.15\) more optimistic leaders enter closer to the
boundary. The credit spread at entry does not follow the distance: under
par debt at \(\ell = 0.40\) it is U-shaped in \(\lambda\) on the grid,
\(96\) basis points at \(\lambda = 0.05\), a minimum of \(32\) near
\(\lambda = 0.30\) and \(34\) at \(0.50\); under fairly priced debt it
is U-shaped as well and higher, \(99\) basis points at
\(\lambda = 0.05\), \(36\) near \(\lambda = 0.30\) and \(37\) at
\(0.50\) (Internet Appendix B).}

Parts (iii) to (v) are statements about computed objects. The
no-deviation conditions are checked at the demand levels of a scan and
by a linear complementarity solve between them, not proved, and the
rent-equalization trigger is the first up-crossing found on a
logarithmic demand scan; I do not show that it is unique, and with the
leader's scale held fixed the gap does cross more than once. Three
things lie outside what is established. The first is uniqueness: the
equilibrium is one in pure strategies in which the firms know their
roles, and nothing here says how the roles are assigned. The second is
the symmetric equilibrium. Between \(X_L\) and \(X_P\) each firm would
rather that the other invest. These are the payoffs of a war of
attrition, not those of the preemption race of
\citet{fudenberg1985preemption}, and a symmetric equilibrium there would
have each firm delay at random. I do not solve it; since both firms want
to lead above \(X_P\), I report \(X_P\) as an upper bound on the date of
first investment. The third is the continuation after a switch that
finds neither firm installed, for which \(W\) takes the symmetric
outcome of the post-switch race. A firm assured of leading that race
would prefer to wait for the switch at every arrival rate on the grid
from \(0.075\) up; its rival would then be the one to invest before the
switch, at the same \(X_L\) (Internet Appendix A). The date \(X_L\)
therefore requires that the firm that invests first not be assured of
leading after the switch. Unless stated otherwise the quantities below
are evaluated for a leader that enters at \(X_L\).

\textbf{Remark 4} (Leverage in the equilibrium). \emph{Under par debt at
\(r_c < r\), higher leverage raises both capacities, both triggers and
the leader's boundary: at \(\ell = 0.40\) against \(\ell = 0\), \(K_L\)
rises by \(70\%\), \(K_F\) by \(70\%\), \(X_L\) by \(37\%\), \(X_F^*\)
by \(37\%\) and the leader's boundary by \(46\%\), and the distance to
default at entry falls from \(4.8\) to \(4.5\). These effects are the
financing subsidy. With the coupon set so that debt is worth its
proceeds at issuance, the same leverage lowers \(K_L\) by \(0.6\%\),
\(K_F\) by \(0.4\%\), \(X_L\) by \(0.4\%\) and \(X_F^*\) by \(0.1\%\),
while the leader's boundary still rises, by \(11\%\), and the distance
to default at entry falls to \(4.3\).} The reason is that under fair
pricing the entry value is firm value less \(I(K)\), which leverage
changes only through the gap between the default boundary and the
shutdown boundary. What survives fair pricing is therefore the effect of
leverage on the boundary and on the distance to default, not its effect
on scale or timing. The ratio \(X_L/X_P\) stays between \(0.39\) and
\(0.40\) at each leverage and under both conventions, and the
no-deviation conditions hold in every leveraged cell solved.

The allocation result has a straightforward reading. Both contests run
over the same installed capacity, scaled by \(\phi\) after the switch
and by \(1 - \phi\) before it, so against a rival at the same allocation
a change in \(\phi\) moves both shares by the same factor and drops out
of the ratio of marginal values. In equilibrium the two firms are not at
the same allocation, because the follower enters after the switch and
has no use for inference capacity, and the leader tilts toward
inference, where it has no rival. The tilt is small at baseline and
larger when the switch is believed to be far.

Figure~\ref{fig-competition-effect} plots the two duopoly triggers
against the single firm's triggers across beliefs. A single firm that
chooses its scale whenever it invests has a finite pre-switch trigger
only for \(\lambda < 0.073\), and that trigger rises without bound as
\(\lambda\) approaches the threshold; above it the firm waits for the
switch (Section~\ref{sec-single-firm}). In the duopoly a firm invests
before the switch at every arrival rate on the grid, at \(X_L\) in the
equilibrium of Proposition 3(iii), with \(X_P\) as an upper bound; the
shaded band between the two is the range in which each firm would rather
that the other invest. Competition is thus what brings investment
forward of the switch at baseline, and the comparison with the single
firm is one of existence, not of a percentage discount. The force at
work is the race that follows the switch and not a race before it: a
firm that waits for the switch enters a post-switch game in which the
rent from leading is competed away, and installing before the switch
secures a position ahead of it, which is why leading at \(X_L\) is worth
more than mutual waiting although following would be better still.
Against the single firm with a precommitted post-switch scale, whose
trigger is always finite, the first investment comes at a demand level
\(64\%\) lower at baseline, and the rent-equalization trigger is
\(7.4\%\) lower. The pure-power approximation reported a discount of
44\% from a trigger of \(0.0026\); in the exact game the first
investment is at \(0.0034\) and rent equalization at \(0.0086\).

\begin{figure}[!htb]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_competition_effect.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-competition-effect}Pre-switch investment triggers of
the single firm and of the duopoly leader as a function of the arrival
rate \(\lambda\), in the exact problem. The solid black line is the
trigger of a single firm that chooses its scale whenever it invests,
which is finite only for \(\lambda < 0.073\); in the shaded region that
firm waits for the switch. The dotted line is the trigger of a single
firm whose post-switch scale is precommitted (Proposition 1(iii)). The
solid line with squares is the first investment \(X_L\) of the duopoly
equilibrium in which one firm leads and the other follows, and the
dashed line with circles the rent-equalization trigger \(X_P\), an upper
bound on the date of first investment; both are solved at ten arrival
rates with the leader's capacity and training fraction chosen at the
entry demand (Proposition 3). Both axes are logarithmic. Zero leverage,
with the option to stop operating; all other parameters at baseline
values.}

\end{figure}%

\textbf{The leader--follower scale asymmetry.} The equilibrium is
asymmetric in scale as well as timing: at baseline the follower installs
\(K_F \approx 0.163\) against the leader's \(K_L \approx 0.0042\), a
ratio of \(39\), and enters at \(X_F^* \approx 0.044\) against
\(X_L \approx 0.0034\), a ratio of \(13\). The direction follows from
the elasticity wedge of part (i), which the capacity first-order
condition amplifies near the (A2) boundary; the magnitude is a joint
property of the contest and of the baseline calibration, not a robust
prediction (Internet Appendix B). The follower's trigger is also remote
in time. Demand reaches it before the switch with probability \(0.4\%\);
it would be reached with probability \(17\%\) if the switch never came,
because the L-regime log drift is negative; and after the switch the
expected passage from \(X_L\) takes \(90\) years, so that the follower's
expected entry date is about \(107\) years after the leader's. The
``leader'' here is therefore not the dominant incumbent of the
AI-rivalry narrative but a small, early commitment, the ``follower'' is
a large entrant whose arrival is a distant event at the horizon of any
investment decision, and the paper's claims about leadership concern the
\emph{timing} margin only. The firm exposed to default in the model is
the leader. At entry its demand is \(4.8\) times its boundary without
debt and \(4.5\) times at \(\ell = 0.40\) under par debt, and it reaches
the boundary before the switch with probability \(9.8\%\) and
\(10.7\%\). This is the exposure Amodei describes, a firm that has
committed to compute ahead of revenue and fails if revenue falls short
\citep{amodei2026dwarkesh}, though at baseline the shortfall required is
large, not modest.

Propositions 1 and 2 are analytical. Proposition 3 combines an
analytical characterization of the follower and of the allocation
condition with numerical findings on the equilibrium itself, the
no-deviation conditions included (Table~\ref{tbl-result-taxonomy}).

\section{Quantitative Calibration}\label{sec-calibration}

The calibration maps the model parameters to the AI infrastructure
sector using company filings, industry reports, and the scaling laws
literature. It is \emph{stylized}: the goal is to discipline the model's
quantitative magnitudes and illustrate cross-sectional heterogeneity,
not to estimate firm-level primitives or test the model empirically. Two
caveats apply throughout. First, the Leland default framework is a
tractable approximation to financial distress risk that fits public-debt
issuers better than VC-backed labs, though the qualitative predictions
apply across financing structures (see Section~\ref{sec-discussion};
Internet Appendix C). Second, the training fractions \(\hat{\phi}\) are
assigned by judgment from incomplete evidence and carry substantial
uncertainty (\(\pm 0.10\)).

\subsection{Demand Process}\label{demand-process}

I use regime-dependent drifts \(\mu_L = 0.01\) and \(\mu_H = 0.06\),
common volatility \(\sigma = 0.25\), and an arrival rate
\(\lambda = 0.10\) per year, implying an expected 10 years to the regime
switch; all four are chosen rather than estimated. The drifts are
\emph{risk-adjusted} (certainty-equivalent) rates, not observed revenue
growth, so the 24--39\% cloud revenue growth in Table~\ref{tbl-sources}
in the Internet Appendix is consistent with a much lower \(\mu_H\). The
demand shifter \(X\) is latent, so no observable series identifies
\(\sigma\); the interior-capacity condition (A2) below admits
\(\sigma \in (0.19, 0.39)\) at baseline, and \(\sigma = 0.25\) sits
inside that window. The baseline \(\lambda\) is a moderate prior: it is
more conservative than the beliefs implied by \citet{amodei2024machines}
(\(\lambda \approx 0.30\)--\(0.50\)) and sits at the lower endpoint of
the range implied by Hassabis's public statements
(\(\lambda \approx 0.10\)--\(0.15\)). That dispersion of beliefs is what
generates the cross-sectional variation analyzed in
Section~\ref{sec-valuation}.

The baseline discount rate \(r = 0.12\) is a risk-adjusted WACC chosen
to represent a frontier AI lab rather than estimated from one firm's
data; as in Section~\ref{sec-model} ({Footnote \ref{fn:wacc}}), \(r\) is
the WACC and \(\mu_s\) is net of a risk premium. It exceeds typical
corporate WACCs because frontier AI labs carry substantial technology
risk and elevated costs of equity (Internet Appendix C). Condition (A2),
\(1/\gamma < (\beta_H - 1)/(\alpha\beta_H) < 1\), admits only
\(r \in (0.097, 0.135)\), so the Google-like (\(0.10\)) and baseline
cases lie inside the window while the OpenAI-like (\(0.14\)),
Anthropic-like (\(0.15\)), and xAI-like (\(0.18\)) archetypes violate
its upper bound. Every trigger and capacity result is therefore computed
at the baseline WACC; archetype-specific WACCs enter only through the
capacity-free inversion
\(\lambda = (\hat{\phi}/(1-\hat{\phi}))^{1-\alpha}(r - \mu_H)\) below
(Internet Appendix B).

\subsection{Technology Parameters}\label{technology-parameters}

The revenue elasticity \(\alpha = 0.40\) governs diminishing returns to
capacity, entering both the L-regime inference contest
(Equation~\ref{eq-contest-L}) and the H-regime training contest
(Equation~\ref{eq-contest-H}). The compute-to-loss power laws in
\citet{kaplan2020scaling} and \citet{hoffmann2022training} motivate a
concave revenue technology but do not measure \(\alpha\): a loss
exponent is not a revenue elasticity, so \(\alpha = 0.40\) is a chosen
value. Condition (A2) admits only \(\alpha \in (0.356, 0.534)\), placing
\(\alpha = 0.40\) near the lower bound, and the trigger and capacity are
highly sensitive to it there, with elasticities of \(+19.7\) and
\(+24.2\) (Table~\ref{tbl-elasticities} in the Internet Appendix). That
sensitivity is why the paper reports ratios and percentage differences
rather than levels. The cost function \(I(K) = cK^\gamma\) with
convexity \(\gamma = 1.50\) captures increasing marginal costs from
power constraints, supply chain bottlenecks, and site preparation, and I
normalize the unit cost \(c = 1.0\).

The operating cost rate \(\delta = 0.03\) is the flow cost of
maintaining installed capacity (power, cooling, maintenance, personnel),
distinct from depreciation of GPU hardware, whose capital cost sits in
\(cK^\gamma\). It is likewise chosen, and nothing substantive turns on
it: \(\delta\) enters only through the total cost
\(b(K) = \delta K/r + cK^\gamma\), so a change in \(\delta\) is
equivalent to a rescaling of the capacity unit and leaves \(\phi^*\),
the trigger ratio, and the Dario's dilemma losses unchanged (Internet
Appendix C). Table~\ref{tbl-parameters} in the Internet Appendix records
the calibration status of every parameter.

\subsection{Stylized Firm Archetypes}\label{stylized-firm-archetypes}

I construct four stylized firm archetypes spanning the cross-sectional
variation in leverage, discount rate, and training allocation. They are
\emph{illustrative composites}, not structural estimates of any specific
company's parameters; Internet Appendix C documents the sourcing
archetype by archetype and Table~\ref{tbl-sources} there lists the
sources. For the three archetypes modelled on privately held firms
(Anthropic-like, OpenAI-like, and xAI-like), the values rest on press
reports rather than audited filings and carry lower confidence than the
Google-like archetype's. Because the underlying revenue and
capital-expenditure concepts are not harmonized, the columns of
Table~\ref{tbl-firms} are model inputs, not measured cross-sectional
moments.
