\subsection*{A. Proofs and Supporting
Derivations}\label{a.-proofs-and-supporting-derivations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{A. Proofs and Supporting Derivations}

Parts of the algebra and single-variable calculus behind Propositions 1
to 3 and Corollary 1 are machine-checked in Lean 4\footnote{\url{https://lean-lang.org/}}
and its mathematical library Mathlib. Each formalized result is an
identity or an inequality under stated hypotheses, conditional on the
functional forms of the value functions, the exercise payoffs, and the
value matching and smooth pasting conditions derived in the proofs
below; none proves that a policy is optimal in its stopping problem,
that an equilibrium exists or is unique, or any number in the paper. For
Proposition 1, the formalization covers the reduction of the homogeneous
Euler ODE to the characteristic equation Equation~\ref{eq-beta-H} (a
power solution \(X^\beta\) solves it if and only if \(Q(\beta) = 0\))
and the sign and ordering of the roots, \(\beta_H > 1\),
\(\beta_L^- < 0\) and \(\beta_L^+ > \beta_H\); for part (i), the
reduction of the allocation first-order condition to its unique interior
root \(\phi^*\) and the comparative statics of \(\phi^*\) (Steps 5 and
6); for part (ii), the trigger and the net present value for a payoff
linear in demand, the equivalence of Equation~\ref{eq-foc-K} with the
closed form Equation~\ref{eq-K-H}, the positivity of that closed form
under (A2), and the sign of the derivative of the reduced capacity
objective of Step 3, which makes \(K_H^*\) its unique global maximizer
(Steps 1 to 4, which with \(a = g(\phi^*)\) also give the pure-power
approximation Equation~\ref{eq-trigger-phi}); and for part (iii), the
particular solutions in Equation~\ref{eq-particular-C} and
Equation~\ref{eq-option-L-upper}, the sign \(Q_L(\beta_H) < 0\), and the
equivalence of the first-order conditions of Step 7 in the trigger and
in capacity with smooth pasting of Equation~\ref{eq-premium} and with
Equation~\ref{eq-scale-foc}. For Proposition 2, it covers the
equivalence of smooth pasting on Equation~\ref{eq-equity} with the
closed-form boundary Equation~\ref{eq-default-boundary}, the shutdown
boundary at a zero coupon and the continuity of the boundary in
leverage, the monotonicity of the boundary in leverage and rival
capacity, the quotient-rule derivative
\(\partial A_{\text{eff}}/\partial \lambda\), the faith-based survival
condition, the threshold Equation~\ref{eq-phi-underbar} and its
comparative statics in \(\alpha\), \(r\) and \(\mu_H - \mu_L\), and, for
\(\tilde{\phi}\), the monotonicity of the markup channel in \(\lambda\)
and the net-condition rearrangement. For the recovery rule
Equation~\ref{eq-recovery}, it covers the following facts at \(\phi^*\):
the inference business earns the firm's revenue per unit of capacity if
and only if the allocation first-order condition holds, so that \(X_I\)
equals the firm's shutdown boundary and \(\Lambda = (1 - \phi^*)U\); and
without the liquidator's option to shut down, the inference business has
positive net value at \(X_D\) if and only if the coupon exceeds
\((1 - M)/M\) times the operating cost, with
\(M = \beta_L^-/(\beta_L^- - 1)\). For Corollary 1, it covers
Equation~\ref{eq-installed-lambda}, part (i) in full (strictly
increasing and strictly concave on \(\lambda \geq 0\) if and only if
\(\phi > \underline{\phi}\), with the converse cases), the identity of
part (ii) and its sign, and, for part (iii),
\(\partial G/\partial \phi > 0\) and the monotonicity of the
semi-elasticity in \(\phi\). For Proposition 3, it covers the follower's
revenue elasticity \(\alpha(2 - s_F)\) and the strict decrease of the
logarithmic derivative of its option coefficient, so that
Equation~\ref{eq-follower-foc} has at most one root (part i); the scale
invariance of the contest share and the contest-derivative factorization
behind the critical-point statement of part (ii); and the algebra of
Equation~\ref{eq-values-before-entry} used in the no-deviation argument
of part (iii), with the values and the maximization that defines \(X_L\)
taken as given. The package also contains an intermediate-value lemma
and a strict-concavity single-crossing lemma that concern only the
nested pure-power game of Internet Appendix B and say nothing about
\(X_L\) or \(X_P\). Each theorem is proved in full, depends only on the
proof assistant's standard logical axioms, and is stated for arbitrary
parameters satisfying its hypotheses rather than for the baseline
calibration alone; \texttt{lean/README.md} in the package maps each
theorem to the result it supports.

The following are not formalized. The stochastic-calculus derivation of
the Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman equations (Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L} and the
equity and debt equations) is taken as given, and so is every
optimal-stopping verification argument: that smooth pasting
characterizes the optimal investment or default rule, which for the
default boundary means that only the algebra of smooth pasting is
formalized and not its optimality, and that a candidate value function
is the value function. In Proposition 1, that \(\phi^*\) is a maximum
and not only a critical point (the Inada limits and the strict concavity
of Step 5), and the two-region system of part (iii) as a whole: the
uniqueness argument that reduces the homogeneous part to a multiple of
\(X^{\beta_L^+}\), the value and slope matching at \(X_H^*\) that pin
down \((A_1, G_1, G_2)\), the existence of a solution, and its single
maximum in capacity, which is a numerical finding. Nor are the waiting
margin Equation~\ref{eq-waiting-margin} and the existence criterion
built on it, the pure-power (\(A_1 = 0\)) form of the L-regime option
value, which is an approximation rather than a theorem (the remark on
the pure-power form after the Proof of Proposition 1 below, and
Section~\ref{sec-conventions}), or, in Proposition 2, the
single-boundary reduction and the sign and size of its bias, the
leverage-training substitution of part (iii), and the magnitudes of
\(m\) and \(\tilde{\phi}\). The caps of the recovery rule, the fair
coupon, and every spread, default probability and recovery level are not
formalized. In Proposition 3, the existence of a root of
Equation~\ref{eq-follower-foc} and the follower's problem with the
option to stop, that the critical point of part (ii) is a maximum, and
the entry equilibrium of parts (iii) to (v) and Remark 4 are not
formalized; neither is Corollary 1 for the claims that carry the option
to stop. Results that are numerical in nature, Numerical Finding 1, the
error of the pure-power approximation, and the coupled-ODE,
piecewise-stopping and entry-game computations of Internet Appendix B,
are likewise not formalized. The Lean proof package accompanies the
submission; the code reproducing the numerical results (Python source,
locked environment, and the commands behind each exhibit) is in the
public repository and will be archived with the paper upon publication.

\subsubsection*{Proof of Proposition 1}\label{proof-of-proposition-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Proof of Proposition 1}

\emph{Maintained assumptions.} Assumption 1. Parts (i) and (ii) use only
the installed values of Section~\ref{sec-single-firm} and the absorbing
H-regime problem. Part (iii) takes Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L} and the value
matching and smooth pasting conditions as given and derives the
conditions an interior pre-switch policy satisfies when the post-switch
scale is precommitted. Steps 1--4 establish part (ii), Steps 5--6 part
(i), and Step 7 part (iii).

\emph{Step 1: Optimal trigger given capacity.} Steps 1--4 solve a
stopping problem with exercise payoff \(a K^\alpha X - \Theta(K)\),
where \(a > 0\) does not depend on \(K\),
\(\Theta(K) = cK^\gamma + \delta K / r\) is the investment outlay plus
the present value of the operating-cost flow, and the continuation value
is the single power \(B X^{\beta_H}\). With \(a = A_H\) this is the
absorbing H-regime problem of a firm that devotes all capacity to
training, which is optimal there because inference capacity earns
nothing after the switch. Value matching and smooth pasting yield: \[
X(K) = \frac{\beta_H}{\beta_H - 1} \cdot \frac{\Theta(K)}{a K^\alpha}.
\]

\emph{Step 2: NPV at the trigger.} The present value of revenue at the
trigger is \(a K^\alpha X(K) = \frac{\beta_H}{\beta_H - 1} \Theta(K)\),
so the NPV at the trigger is: \[
V - I = \frac{1}{\beta_H - 1}\, \Theta(K).
\]

\emph{Step 3: Reduced capacity objective.} Substituting the trigger and
NPV into the option value \(F(X_0) = (V - I)(X_0/X(K))^{\beta_H}\): \[
F(X_0) = \mathcal{C} \cdot \frac{(a K^\alpha)^{\beta_H}}{\Theta(K)^{\beta_H - 1}} \equiv \mathcal{C} \cdot h(K),
\] where
\(\mathcal{C} = \frac{1}{\beta_H - 1}\left(\frac{(\beta_H - 1) X_0}{\beta_H}\right)^{\beta_H} > 0\)
is independent of \(K\). The optimal capacity maximizes \(h(K)\).

\emph{Step 4: First-order condition for \(K\).} Setting
\((\partial \ln h / \partial K) = 0\):
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-foc-K}{
\frac{\alpha \beta_H}{K} = \frac{(\beta_H - 1)(c\gamma K^{\gamma - 1} + \delta / r)}{cK^\gamma + \delta K / r}.
}\end{equation} The coefficient \(a\) has dropped out, so the solution
depends only on the cost and technology parameters
\((\alpha, \beta_H, \gamma, \delta, r, c)\). Solving for \(K\) yields
the closed form Equation~\ref{eq-K-H}. The critical point is the global
maximum. The function
\(h \propto K^{\alpha\beta_H} / (cK^\gamma + \delta K/r)^{\beta_H - 1}\)
is continuous and strictly positive on \((0, \infty)\). As \(K \to 0\)
the linear term dominates the denominator and
\(h \sim K^{\alpha\beta_H - (\beta_H - 1)} \to 0\), because the upper
bound in (A2) gives \(\alpha\beta_H > \beta_H - 1\). As \(K \to \infty\)
the convex term dominates and
\(h \sim K^{\alpha\beta_H - \gamma(\beta_H - 1)} \to 0\), because the
lower bound in (A2) gives \(\alpha\beta_H < \gamma(\beta_H - 1)\). A
positive continuous function vanishing at both ends attains an interior
maximum, and Equation~\ref{eq-foc-K} is linear in \(K^{\gamma - 1}\)
with a single root, so that root is it. Both limits require
\(\delta > 0\): at \(\delta = 0\) the denominator is \(cK^\gamma\)
alone, \(h \sim K^{\alpha\beta_H - \gamma(\beta_H - 1)}\) is
monotonically decreasing on the whole half-line, and no interior optimum
exists (the closed form returns zero), which is why (A1) imposes
\(\delta > 0\). The same four steps with \(a = g(\phi)\), the
coefficient of \(A_{\text{eff}} = g(\phi) K^\alpha\), are the pure-power
approximation of Section~\ref{sec-single-firm}, which is why its
capacity is \(K_H^*\) whatever the allocation and the arrival rate, and
why its trigger Equation~\ref{eq-trigger-phi} is
\(X_H^* A_H / g(\phi^*)\).

\emph{Step 5: Existence of interior \(\phi^*\).}

Write
\(A_{\text{eff}}(\phi) = w_L \cdot [(1-\phi)K]^\alpha + w_H \cdot (\phi K)^\alpha\),
where \(w_L = 1/(r - \mu_L + \lambda) > 0\) and
\(w_H = \lambda A_H / (r - \mu_L + \lambda) > 0\). The payoff from
investing \((K, \phi)\) at demand \(X\) before the switch is
\(A_{\text{eff}}(\phi, K) X - \Theta(K)\), and neither \(\Theta(K)\) nor
the continuation value of a firm that has not invested depends on
\(\phi\). At every capacity and every trigger, the optimal allocation
therefore maximizes \(A_{\text{eff}}(\phi)\).

The first derivative with respect to \(\phi\) is: \[
\frac{\partial A_{\text{eff}}}{\partial \phi} = \alpha K^\alpha \bigl[-w_L (1-\phi)^{\alpha - 1} + w_H \phi^{\alpha - 1}\bigr].
\]

\emph{Boundary behavior.} Since \(\alpha \in (0,1)\), the exponent
\(\alpha - 1 < 0\), so:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  As \(\phi \to 0^+\): \(\phi^{\alpha - 1} \to +\infty\) while
  \((1-\phi)^{\alpha-1} \to 1\), so
  \(\partial A_{\text{eff}}/\partial \phi \to +\infty\). (Inada
  condition: the first unit of training has infinite marginal value.)
\item
  As \(\phi \to 1^-\): \((1-\phi)^{\alpha - 1} \to +\infty\) while
  \(\phi^{\alpha-1} \to 1\), so
  \(\partial A_{\text{eff}}/\partial \phi \to -\infty\). (Inada
  condition: the first unit of inference has infinite marginal value.)
\end{itemize}

Since \(\partial A_{\text{eff}}/\partial \phi\) is continuous on
\((0,1)\), positive near \(\phi = 0\), and negative near \(\phi = 1\),
by the intermediate value theorem there exists \(\phi^* \in (0,1)\) with
\(\partial A_{\text{eff}}/\partial \phi = 0\).

\emph{Uniqueness.} The second derivative is: \[
\frac{\partial^2 A_{\text{eff}}}{\partial \phi^2} = \alpha(\alpha - 1) K^\alpha \bigl[w_L (1-\phi)^{\alpha - 2} + w_H \phi^{\alpha - 2}\bigr] < 0
\] for all \(\phi \in (0,1)\), since \(\alpha \in (0,1)\) implies
\(\alpha(\alpha-1) < 0\) while both bracketed terms
\((1-\phi)^{\alpha - 2}\) and \(\phi^{\alpha - 2}\) are positive. Strict
concavity of \(A_{\text{eff}}\) in \(\phi\) guarantees uniqueness.

\emph{Interior solution.} Setting
\(\partial A_{\text{eff}} / \partial \phi = 0\) yields: \[
\frac{w_H}{w_L} = \left(\frac{1-\phi^*}{\phi^*}\right)^{\alpha - 1},
\] which uniquely determines \(\phi^*\) as an increasing function of
\(w_H / w_L\).

\emph{Step 6: Comparative statics.} Because \(A_{\text{eff}}\) is
strictly concave in \(\phi\) with interior maximizer \(\phi^*\), and
\(\phi^*\) is increasing in \(w_H/w_L\) (Step 5):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\partial \phi^* / \partial \lambda > 0\): Higher \(\lambda\)
  increases \(w_H / w_L = \lambda A_H\). Since \(\phi^*\) is increasing
  in \(w_H / w_L\), the optimal training fraction rises. (ii)
  \(\partial \phi^* / \partial \mu_H > 0\): Higher \(\mu_H\) lowers
  \(r - \mu_H\), increasing \(w_H / w_L = \lambda / (r - \mu_H)\). Since
  \(\phi^*\) is increasing in \(w_H / w_L\), the optimal training
  fraction rises. Note that \(\phi^*\) is independent of \(\mu_L\): both
  \(w_L = 1/(r - \mu_L + \lambda)\) and
  \(w_H = \lambda A_H / (r - \mu_L + \lambda)\) share the denominator
  \((r - \mu_L + \lambda)\), so
  \(w_H / w_L = \lambda A_H = \lambda / (r - \mu_H)\) does not depend on
  \(\mu_L\).
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Step 7: The pre-switch policy with precommitted post-switch
scale.} Let \(S(X)\) denote the value of never investing before the
switch and installing \(K_H^*\) after it. It solves
Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L} on the whole half-line:
\(S(X) = A_w X^{\beta_L^+} + \Gamma X^{\beta_H}\) below \(X_H^*\) and
\(S(X) = G_w X^{\beta_L^-} + [\lambda p_H/(r + \lambda - \mu_L)] X - \lambda q_H/(r + \lambda)\)
above it, where the no-bubble condition removes \(X^{\beta_L^+}\) from
the upper branch and \((A_w, G_w)\) are pinned by value and slope
matching at \(X_H^*\). Consider the policy of installing \((K, \phi^*)\)
when demand first reaches \(\bar{X}\), with payoff
\(P(X; K) = g(\phi^*) K^\alpha X - \Theta(K)\). Below \(\bar{X}\) its
value differs from \(S\) by a solution of the homogeneous equation that
vanishes at the origin, so
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-premium}{
F_L(X) = S(X) + \bigl[P(\bar{X}; K) - S(\bar{X})\bigr] \left(\frac{X}{\bar{X}}\right)^{\beta_L^+}, \quad X < \bar{X}.
}\end{equation} The firm maximizes the coefficient
\([P(\bar{X}; K) - S(\bar{X})]\,\bar{X}^{-\beta_L^+}\), which does not
depend on the demand level at which it stands. The first-order condition
in \(\bar{X}\) is
\(\bar{X}\,[g(\phi^*) K^\alpha - S'(\bar{X})] = \beta_L^+ [P(\bar{X}; K) - S(\bar{X})]\),
which is smooth pasting of Equation~\ref{eq-premium} against the payoff,
value matching holding by construction. The first-order condition in
\(K\) is \(\partial P / \partial K = 0\) at \(\bar{X}\), which is
Equation~\ref{eq-scale-foc}. Writing out Equation~\ref{eq-premium} on
the two sides of \(X_H^*\) gives Equation~\ref{eq-option-L-general} with
\(A_1 = A_w + [P - S]\,\bar{X}^{-\beta_L^+}\) and
Equation~\ref{eq-option-L-upper} with
\(G_1 = [P - S]\,\bar{X}^{-\beta_L^+}\) and \(G_2 = G_w\), and the value
and slope matching at \(X_H^*\) is inherited from \(S\). These are the
conditions of part (iii). They are necessary for an interior optimum;
that the coefficient has a single maximum in \(K\) is verified
numerically (Internet Appendix B), not proved. At baseline
\(A_w \approx -1.18 \times 10^4\) and \(A_1 \approx -0.85 \times 10^4\).
\(\square\)

\emph{Remark on the pure-power form of \(F_L\) (approximation).}

This remark examines the pure-power approximation of
Section~\ref{sec-single-firm} against the exact solution of Step 7.

The general L-regime option value
\(F_L(X) = A_1 X^{\beta_L^+} + \Gamma X^{\beta_H}\)
(Equation~\ref{eq-option-L-general}) decomposes into a homogeneous
component \(A_1 X^{\beta_L^+}\), where \(\beta_L^+\) is the positive
root of the homogeneous L-regime switching ODE (hazard-adjusted discount
rate \(r + \lambda\); Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L} without its forcing term
\(\lambda B_H X^{\beta_H}\)), and a particular component
\(\Gamma X^{\beta_H}\), driven by the forcing term \(\lambda F_H(X)\),
with coefficient \(\Gamma = -\lambda B_H / Q_L(\beta_H)\) determined by
the ODE (Equation~\ref{eq-particular-C}). The coefficient is positive:
evaluating \(Q_L\) at the H-regime root gives
\(Q_L(\beta_H) = (\mu_L - \mu_H)\beta_H - \lambda < 0\), which also
places \(\beta_H\) strictly below the larger root \(\beta_L^+\), since
\(Q_L\) is an upward parabola. Two qualifications bear on this
decomposition; the second is the substantive one.

\emph{Range of validity of the forcing term.} The forcing term
\(\lambda F_H(X)\) takes the option form \(\lambda B_H X^{\beta_H}\)
only for \(X < X_H^*\). For \(X > X_H^*\) a switch to the H-regime is
followed by immediate exercise, so the forcing term is the exercised
value. At the scale \(K_H^*\) chosen for entry at \(X_H^*\) it is
\(\lambda [V_H(X, K_H^*, \phi_H^*) - I(K_H^*)]\), affine in \(X\); a
firm that has not invested when the switch arrives is not bound to that
scale, and its payoff is the envelope
\(\lambda \max_K [V_H(X, K, \phi_H^*) - I(K)]\), which is tangent to the
affine payoff at \(X_H^*\) and lies above it for \(X > X_H^*\) (by
13.6\% at \(X = 2X_H^*\) at baseline). In either case the continuation
value solves a piecewise problem with value and slope matched at
\(X_H^*\); Internet Appendix B solves the precommitted-scale version in
closed form and the unrestricted version numerically. Accordingly,
Equation~\ref{eq-particular-C} is the exact particular solution on
\((0, X_H^*)\); above \(X_H^*\) the pure power form extends it. At the
baseline calibration both the exact trigger \(X^* \approx 0.0093\) and
the approximate trigger \(X^{\text{pp}} \approx 0.0047\) exceed
\(X_H^* \approx 0.0028\), so this interval is non-empty.

\emph{Sign of the homogeneous coefficient.} The homogeneous term is the
value of being able to exercise \emph{before} the regime switch, using
the combined revenue coefficient. The L-regime insufficiency condition
Equation~\ref{eq-l-insufficiency} does not eliminate it. What that
condition rules out is an interior optimal \emph{scale} for the
standalone, no-switching L-regime problem: at a fixed capacity \(K\)
that problem has the finite trigger
\(X_L^{\text{pure}}(K) = [\beta_L^0/(\beta_L^0 - 1)] \cdot [\delta K/r + I(K)]/(A_L K^\alpha)\)
with \(A_L = 1/(r - \mu_L)\) and \(\beta_L^0 \approx 2.33\) the positive
root of the characteristic equation discounted at \(r\) (no switching),
and an interior optimum over \(K\) requires the option premium ratio
\((1 - 1/\beta_L^0)/\alpha < 1\) (the L-regime analogue of condition
(A2): the marginal value of capacity, \(\alpha\), must be large enough
relative to the markup-adjusted payout share). At baseline that ratio is
\(1.43\), and the hazard-adjusted version in
Equation~\ref{eq-l-insufficiency},
\(\Phi_L = (1 - 1/\beta_L^+)/\alpha \approx 1.67\) with \(\beta_L^+\)
the root at \(r + \lambda\), is larger still; either way the standalone
L-regime scale problem has no interior optimum. That is a statement
about the absence of an optimal scale, not about profitability: small
standalone L-regime projects have positive value at positive demand, and
the option factor diverges as \(K \to 0\), which is exactly why no scale
is optimal. It does not follow that the coefficient on the homogeneous
root vanishes. Nor can the sign be settled by the replication argument
that suggests itself, namely that the firm can always follow the pure
switching strategy (never exercise in regime \(L\), wait for the
H-regime arrival, and then hold the H-regime option) and that this
strategy is worth \(\Gamma X^{\beta_H}\). It is not: the pure switching
strategy faces the same piecewise forcing as every other strategy, so
its value is \(A_w X^{\beta_L^+} + \Gamma X^{\beta_H}\) below \(X_H^*\)
and
\(G_w X^{\beta_L^-} + [\lambda p_H/(r + \lambda - \mu_L)] X - \lambda q_H/(r + \lambda)\)
above it, where \(p_H\) and \(q_H\) are the slope and intercept of the
exercised H-regime payoff (Internet Appendix B) and \((A_w, G_w)\) are
pinned by value and slope matching at \(X_H^*\) once the no-bubble
condition removes \(X^{\beta_L^+}\) from the upper branch. The exercised
H-regime payoff lies strictly below the extrapolated power
\(B_H X^{\beta_H}\) for \(X > X_H^*\), since the two are tangent at
\(X_H^*\) and the power is convex, so the true forcing is dominated
pointwise by the forcing Equation~\ref{eq-particular-C} assumes, and
\(A_w < 0\): at the baseline calibration
\(A_w \approx -1.18 \times 10^4\), so the pure switching value lies
\emph{below} \(\Gamma X^{\beta_H}\) everywhere on \((0, X_H^*)\), by
10\% at \(X = X_H^*\). The valid statements are therefore that
\(A_1 > A_w\), since exercising before the switch is worth something,
and that \(\Gamma X^{\beta_H}\) is an upper, not a lower, bound on the
precommitted-scale continuation value below \(X_H^*\), the latter
because the extrapolated forcing dominates the true one pointwise and,
at the baseline calibration, the fictitious problem with that forcing
has no finite exercise boundary at all (Internet Appendix B). Solving
the precommitted-scale problem (Step 7, Internet Appendix B) gives
\(A_1 \approx -0.85 \times 10^4 < 0\) at the exact policy and
\(-1.01 \times 10^4\) at the capacity of the approximation. Neither
\(A_1 = 0\) nor \(A_1 \geq 0\) can be derived from
Equation~\ref{eq-l-insufficiency}. At the baseline calibration,
\(\beta_L^+ \approx 3.01\) gives \(\Phi_L \approx 1.67\), so the
condition holds with a wide margin.

\emph{The approximation.} Imposing \(A_1 = 0\) and pricing the
pre-investment option with the pure power form
\(F_L(X) \propto X^{\beta_H}\) on the whole continuation region gives
the pure-power approximation. It is the unconditional-\(A_{\text{eff}}\)
convention of Section~\ref{sec-conventions} applied to the single firm:
option-like L-regime payoffs are discounted with the H-regime exponent
\(\beta_H\) in place of a regime-switching first-passage factor mixing
\(X^{\beta_L^+}\) and \(X^{\beta_H}\), and the exercise payoff is the
unconditional L-regime installed value. With a single power in the
continuation region, dividing value-matching by smooth pasting at the
trigger eliminates the coefficient and yields: \[
X^{\text{pp}} = \frac{\beta_H}{\beta_H - 1} \cdot \frac{\delta K_H^*/r + I(K_H^*)}{A_{\text{eff}}(\phi^*, K_H^*)},
\] which is Equation~\ref{eq-trigger-phi} (Steps 1--4 with
\(a = g(\phi^*)\)). The trigger depends only on the
value-matching/smooth-pasting ratio, not on the level of the
coefficient, which value-matching then determines; the two boundary
conditions pin down the trigger and the level jointly. The price is that
this smooth-fit level differs from the forced-ODE value \(\Gamma\) of
Equation~\ref{eq-particular-C}: at the baseline calibration the
smooth-fit coefficient is
\([V(X^{\text{pp}}, K_H^*, \phi^*) - I(K_H^*)]/(X^{\text{pp}})^{\beta_H} \approx 16.5\)
against \(\Gamma \approx 21.1\), so the candidate does not solve
Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L} on any interval and is a decision rule, not a
value function. The direction of the resulting error is not implied by
that comparison; Internet Appendix B measures it against the exact
solution. At the baseline calibration and at the approximate policy, the
precommitted-scale continuation value exceeds the smooth-fit value at
every demand level while remaining below \(\Gamma X^{\beta_H}\), so the
approximation understates the pre-investment option value, by 19\% at
\(X_0 = X^{\text{pp}}/2\). The ordering is not general: it reverses for
\(\lambda \leq 0.02\). The comparison shows what the approximation
preserves and what it does not. Its levels \(X^{\text{pp}}\) and
\(K^{\text{pp}}\) are not estimates of the exact policy, whose trigger
and capacity are about twice as large. The training fraction \(\phi^*\)
is identical in the two. And the value delivered by the approximate
policy is within about 3\% of the value of the exact policy of
Proposition 1(iii) and within about 7\% of the unrestricted optimum,
which at baseline is to wait for the switch (Internet Appendix B).

\subsubsection*{Proof of Proposition 2}\label{proof-of-proposition-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Proof of Proposition 2}

\emph{Maintained assumptions.} Assumption 1(A1). Capacity
\((K_i, \phi_i)\) and rival \((K_j, \phi_j)\) are held fixed at
arbitrary values with \(K_i, K_j > 0\) and
\(\phi_i, \phi_j \in (0, 1)\), as in the statement; no step uses their
optimality, except the paragraph on the sign at the optimal allocation,
which is labelled as such. The default boundary follows from the
\citet{leland1994corporate} framework adapted to regime switching.

\emph{One-way coupling and the single-boundary reduction.} In a general
regime-switching credit model, equity values \((E_L, E_H)\) satisfy
coupled ODEs with potentially regime-contingent default boundaries
\((X_D^L, X_D^H)\). One feature of the model and one approximation
together permit a tractable single-boundary reduction.

The feature is that the regime switch is absorbing (\(L \to H\) only, no
\(H \to L\)), so the H-regime equity \(E_H(X)\) satisfies a standard
(uncoupled) Leland ODE with revenue coefficient
\(A_H (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha s_i^H\) and closed-form solution
\(E_H(X) = A_H (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha s_i^H X - (C_D + \delta K_i)/r + D_H X^{\beta_H^-}\),
whose last term is the default option (put-like, with \(\beta_H^- < 0\)
and \(D_H > 0\)). The L-regime equity \(E_L(X)\) is then forced by
\(\lambda E_H(X)\), a one-way coupling.

The approximation is to replace \(E_H\) with its perpetuity component:
\[
E_H(X) \approx A_H (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha \cdot s_i^H \cdot X - C_D/r - \delta K_i/r,
\] after which the L-regime equity ODE yields a particular solution with
coefficient \(A_{\text{eff},i}\), and Leland's smooth pasting gives the
default boundary below in closed form. The omitted term
\(D_H X^{\beta_H^-}\) is the (positive) value of the H-regime default
option, so the perpetuity approximation understates the H-regime
continuation value and the closed-form boundary weakly \emph{overstates}
\(X_D\). The magnitude of the bias is computed by solving the coupled
system exactly: substituting the closed-form \(E_H\) (including
\(D_H X^{\beta_H^-}\)) into the L-regime ODE yields an additional
particular term \(C_2 X^{\beta_H^-}\) with
\(C_2 = -\lambda D_H / Q_L(\beta_H^-)\), and value matching plus smooth
pasting at \(X_D\) then determine the boundary jointly with the
homogeneous coefficient. At the baseline calibration the closed-form
boundary exceeds the coupled-system boundary by approximately 3\%,
uniformly across leverage levels (Internet Appendix B). The
approximation therefore errs against the faith-based survival mechanism,
making the levered firm look slightly riskier than the coupled system
implies, and is conservative for the credit-risk conclusions.

In the L-regime, the going-concern equity \(E(X)\) of
Equation~\ref{eq-equity} satisfies an ODE analogous to
Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L}, with the firm defaulting when \(E(X_D) = 0\)
and \(E'(X_D) = 0\) (smooth pasting); the sunk equity contribution
enters the entry NPV \(E(X) - (1-\ell)I(K)\) but not the boundary
conditions. The resulting default boundary is: \[
X_D = \frac{\beta_L^-}{\beta_L^- - 1} \cdot \frac{C_D/r + \delta K_i/r}{A_{\text{eff},i}},
\] where \(\beta_L^-\) is the negative characteristic root,
\(C_D = r_c \cdot \ell \cdot I(K)\) is the coupon, and all comparative
statics below hold \emph{with \((K_i, \phi_i)\) and \((K_j, \phi_j)\)
held fixed}. Nothing in the derivation requires \(C_D > 0\): at
\(\ell = 0\) the same value-matching and smooth-pasting conditions,
applied to the unlevered claim, give the shutdown boundary
\([\beta_L^-/(\beta_L^- - 1)]\,(\delta K_i/r)/A_{\text{eff},i}\), which
is positive under (A1). Parts (ii) to (iv) therefore hold for
\(\ell \geq 0\), and so does part (i) in \(\ell\).

\emph{(i) Default boundary increasing in leverage.} \(X_D\) depends on
\(\ell\) only through the numerator via
\(C_D = r_c \cdot \ell \cdot I(K)\), which is strictly increasing in
\(\ell\). Since \(A_{\text{eff},i}\) is independent of \(\ell\) (holding
capacity and training fixed), \(\partial X_D / \partial \ell > 0\).

\emph{(ii) Default boundary decreasing in \(\lambda\) (faith-based
survival).} Holding \((K_i, \phi_i, K_j, \phi_j)\) fixed, define the
L-regime revenue flow
\(\Pi_L \equiv [(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha \cdot s_i^L\) and the H-regime
revenue present value
\(\Pi_H \equiv (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha \cdot s_i^H / (r - \mu_H)\), both
positive. The effective revenue coefficient is: \[
A_{\text{eff},i} = \frac{\Pi_L + \lambda \cdot \Pi_H}{r - \mu_L + \lambda}.
\] Differentiating with respect to \(\lambda\): \[
\frac{\partial A_{\text{eff},i}}{\partial \lambda} = \frac{\Pi_H(r - \mu_L) - \Pi_L}{(r - \mu_L + \lambda)^2}.
\] This is positive whenever \(\Pi_H(r - \mu_L) > \Pi_L\), i.e., when
the H-regime revenue present value scaled by the L-regime net discount
rate \(r - \mu_L\) exceeds the L-regime revenue flow:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-faith-condition}{
\frac{(\phi_i K_i)^\alpha \cdot s_i^H}{r - \mu_H} \cdot (r - \mu_L) > [(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha \cdot s_i^L.
}\end{equation} Since \(\mu_H > \mu_L\) implies
\((r - \mu_L)/(r - \mu_H) > 1\), the factor \((r - \mu_L)\) amplifies
the H-regime term, making the condition easier to satisfy. Intuitively,
the condition requires that the training fraction \(\phi_i\) is large
enough that the H-regime continuation value contributes meaningfully to
\(A_{\text{eff},i}\).

\emph{Closed-form threshold (symmetric duopoly).} In the symmetric case
with \(s_i^L = s_i^H = 1/2\) and equal capacity, the contest shares and
\(K^\alpha\) terms cancel, and the condition reduces to
\((r - \mu_L)/(r - \mu_H) \cdot \phi^\alpha > (1-\phi)^\alpha\), i.e.,
\((\phi/(1-\phi))^\alpha > (r-\mu_H)/(r-\mu_L)\). Solving for \(\phi\):
\[
\phi > \underline{\phi} = \frac{\Omega}{1 + \Omega}, \quad \Omega = \left(\frac{r - \mu_H}{r - \mu_L}\right)^{1/\alpha}.
\] For the baseline calibration (\(r = 0.12\), \(\mu_H = 0.06\),
\(\mu_L = 0.01\), \(\alpha = 0.40\)):
\(\Omega = (0.06/0.11)^{2.5} \approx 0.22\), giving
\(\underline{\phi} \approx 0.18\).

\emph{Markup channel.} To complete the proof, note that \(X_D\) also
depends on \(\lambda\) through the markup factor
\(M(\beta_L^-) \equiv \beta_L^- / (\beta_L^- - 1)\), since \(\beta_L^-\)
is the negative root of
\(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 \beta(\beta - 1) + \mu_L \beta - (r + \lambda) = 0\)
and thus depends on \(\lambda\). The full derivative decomposes into two
opposing channels: \[
\frac{\partial X_D}{\partial \lambda} = \underbrace{\frac{\partial M}{\partial \lambda} \cdot \frac{N}{A_{\text{eff},i}}}_{>0\;\text{($\beta$-channel)}} + \underbrace{M(\beta_L^-) \cdot N \cdot \frac{\partial (1/A_{\text{eff},i})}{\partial \lambda}}_{<0\;\text{($A_{\text{eff}}$-channel)}},
\] where \(N = C_D/r + \delta K_i/r > 0\). The \(\beta\)-channel is
positive because increasing \(\lambda\) raises the hazard-adjusted
discount rate \((r + \lambda)\), making \(\beta_L^-\) more negative and
pushing the markup \(M\) toward 1 from below (since \(\beta_L^- < 0\)
implies \(M \in (0,1)\)):
\(dM/d\lambda = 1/[(\beta_L^- - 1)^2 \mathcal{R}] > 0\), where
\(\mathcal{R} = \sqrt{(\mu_L - \sigma^2/2)^2 + 2\sigma^2(r+\lambda)}\)
is the discriminant of the L-regime characteristic equation. The
\(A_{\text{eff}}\)-channel is negative under
Equation~\ref{eq-faith-condition}.

\emph{Exact net threshold.} The net sign resolves in closed form. In log
form,
\(\partial \ln X_D / \partial \lambda = m - \partial \ln A_{\text{eff},i} / \partial \lambda\),
where the markup semi-elasticity is
\(m \equiv (\mathrm{d}M/\mathrm{d}\lambda)/M = 1/[\beta_L^-(\beta_L^- - 1)\mathcal{R}] > 0\),
and, from the derivative above, \[
\frac{\partial \ln A_{\text{eff},i}}{\partial \lambda} = \frac{\Pi_H(r - \mu_L) - \Pi_L}{(\Pi_L + \lambda \Pi_H)\,\Delta}, \qquad \Delta \equiv r - \mu_L + \lambda,
\] with \(\Delta\) the hazard-adjusted net discount rate of the
L-regime. The condition \(\partial X_D / \partial \lambda < 0\) is
therefore \(\Pi_H(r - \mu_L) - \Pi_L > m(\Pi_L + \lambda \Pi_H)\Delta\),
which is \emph{linear} in \((\Pi_L, \Pi_H)\) and rearranges to \[
\frac{\Pi_H}{\Pi_L} > \frac{1 + m\Delta}{(r - \mu_L) - m\lambda\Delta},
\] provided the regularity condition \((r - \mu_L) > m\lambda\Delta\)
holds (it does throughout the calibration ranges; if it fails, no
allocation can deliver \(\partial X_D/\partial\lambda < 0\)). With
symmetric shares,
\(\Pi_H/\Pi_L = (\phi/(1-\phi))^\alpha / (r - \mu_H)\), giving the exact
threshold \(\tilde{\phi}\) of Equation~\ref{eq-phi-tilde}. Since
\(m > 0\), \(\tilde{\Omega} > \Omega\) and
\(\tilde{\phi} > \underline{\phi}\); as \(m \to 0\) (no markup channel),
\(\tilde{\phi} \to \underline{\phi}\). At the baseline calibration
\(m \approx 0.77\) and \(\tilde{\phi} \approx 0.32\), between
\(\underline{\phi} \approx 0.18\) and \(\phi^* \approx 0.70\); the
numerically measured \(\beta\)-channel is about one-third of the
\(A_{\text{eff}}\)-channel at \(\phi^*\), consistent with the closed
form. The sign flip of \(\partial X_D/\partial \lambda\) at
\(\tilde{\phi}\) is verified by finite differences.

\emph{Sign at the optimal allocation.} Because \(\tilde{\phi}\) depends
on \(\lambda\) while \(\phi^*(\lambda)\) is increasing in \(\lambda\)
(Proposition 1), the comparison
\(\phi^*(\lambda) \gtrless \tilde{\phi}(\lambda)\) determines the sign
of \(\partial X_D/\partial \lambda\) at the optimum. At the baseline
calibration the two cross at \(\bar{\lambda} \approx 0.034\): for
\(\lambda > \bar{\lambda}\) (including the baseline \(\lambda = 0.10\)
and the full range used in Proposition 3(v)), \(\phi^* > \tilde{\phi}\)
and the default boundary falls with optimism; for
\(\lambda < \bar{\lambda}\), the optimal allocation is too
inference-heavy and \(X_D\) is locally \emph{increasing} in \(\lambda\)
at the optimum, a refinement that the closed form makes visible and that
finite-difference checks confirm (e.g., at \(\lambda = 0.02\),
\(\phi^* \approx 0.14 < \tilde{\phi} \approx 0.27\) and
\(\partial X_D/\partial\lambda > 0\)).

\emph{(iii) Leverage-training substitution.} Write the default boundary
as \(X_D = \Psi(\ell) / A_{\text{eff},i}(\phi_i)\), where
\(\Psi(\ell) = [\beta_L^- / (\beta_L^- - 1)] \cdot (r_c \ell I(K) / r + \delta K/r)\)
is strictly increasing in leverage (note: \(\Psi\) absorbs the markup
factor \(M\), unlike the cost term \(N\) in the
\(\lambda\)-decomposition above). In the monopoly and common-allocation
cases the contest shares are constant in \(\phi_i\), so
\(A_{\text{eff},i}(\phi_i)\) is the single-firm coefficient of
Proposition 1 (Step 5) up to a positive factor: strictly concave in
\(\phi_i\) with the interior maximizer \(\phi^*\), increasing on
\((0, \phi^*)\) and decreasing on \((\phi^*, 1)\). Against a rival at a
different allocation the shares \(s_i^L\) and \(s_i^H\) themselves
depend on \(\phi_i\), and Step 5 does not apply: the coefficient remains
single-peaked numerically, but its maximizer moves with the rival's
allocation (at equal baseline capacities under the Tullock contest, from
about \(0.84\) when \(\phi_j = 0.10\) to \(0.60\) when
\(\phi_j = 0.95\); under the arithmetic-mean industry-revenue benchmark
of Internet Appendix E it is \(\phi^*\) for every \(\phi_j\)). Part
(iii) is therefore stated relative to the maximizer of
\(A_{\text{eff},i}(\cdot)\) given \((K_j, \phi_j)\), which is \(\phi^*\)
in the symmetric and monopoly cases. For \(\phi_i < \phi^*\), a firm can
maintain a given default boundary \(X_D\) while increasing leverage
\(\ell\) (raising the numerator) by simultaneously increasing training
allocation \(\phi_i\) (raising \(A_{\text{eff},i}\) in the denominator).
For \(\phi_i > \phi^*\), the relationship reverses: further increases in
\(\phi_i\) would \emph{lower} \(A_{\text{eff},i}\) and \emph{raise} the
default boundary, so no such substitution is available. The economically
relevant range lies below the maximizer (baseline
\(\phi^* \approx 0.70\) in the symmetric case), where the substitution
applies. Note that this is a \emph{mechanical} relationship describing
the iso-\(X_D\) locus, not a statement about optimality: higher \(\phi\)
sacrifices L-regime revenue, and the net effect on equity value is
ambiguous. Leverage affects the firm through the coupon obligation
(numerator), while training allocation affects the firm through the
expected revenue (denominator).

\emph{(iv) Default boundary increasing in rival capacity.} Both contest
shares
\(s_i^L = [(1-\phi_i)K_i]^\alpha / \sum_j [(1-\phi_j)K_j]^\alpha\) and
\(s_i^H = (\phi_i K_i)^\alpha / \sum_j (\phi_j K_j)^\alpha\) are
decreasing in \(K_j\) (holding \(K_i\) fixed). Since
\(A_{\text{eff},i}\) is increasing in both contest shares,
\(\partial A_{\text{eff},i} / \partial K_j < 0\), and
\(\partial X_D / \partial K_j > 0\): stronger rivals raise the default
boundary. \(\square\)

\subsubsection*{Installed value in the arrival rate (Corollary
1)}\label{installed-value-in-the-arrival-rate-corollary-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Installed value in the arrival rate
(Corollary 1)}

Testable prediction 3 of Section~\ref{sec-discussion} concerns a firm
that has already invested and cannot change its capacity, allocation or
coupon when beliefs about the switch change. Its object is therefore not
the value of the unexercised option in
Figure~\ref{fig-lambda-option-value}, which re-optimizes the policy at
each \(\lambda\), but installed value at a fixed policy.

\textbf{Corollary 1} (Installed value in \(\lambda\)). \emph{Hold a
single installed firm's capacity \(K > 0\), allocation
\(\phi \in (0, 1)\), coupon \(C_D \geq 0\) and current demand \(X > 0\)
fixed, and let \(\lambda \geq 0\) vary, with
\(\Pi_L = [(1-\phi)K]^\alpha\), \(\Pi_H = (\phi K)^\alpha/(r - \mu_H)\),
\(\Delta = r - \mu_L + \lambda\) and
\(G \equiv \Pi_H (r - \mu_L) - \Pi_L\). The going-concern value
\(V(\lambda) = A_{\text{eff}}(\lambda) X - (\delta K + C_D)/r\), the
installed value of Equation~\ref{eq-installed-value-L} net of the
default-free coupon claim and without the option to stop, satisfies}
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-installed-lambda}{
V'(\lambda) = \frac{X G}{\Delta^2}, \qquad V''(\lambda) = -\frac{2 X G}{\Delta^3}.
}\end{equation} \emph{(i) \(V\) is strictly increasing and strictly
concave in \(\lambda\) on all of \(\lambda \geq 0\) if and only if
\(\phi > \underline{\phi}\) of Equation~\ref{eq-phi-underbar}; it is
strictly decreasing and strictly convex if \(\phi < \underline{\phi}\)
and constant at \(\phi = \underline{\phi}\). (ii) For news of size
\(h \in (0, \Delta)\),
\([V(\lambda) - V(\lambda - h)] - [V(\lambda + h) - V(\lambda)] = 2h^2 X G/[\Delta(\Delta - h)(\Delta + h)]\),
which is positive if and only if \(\phi > \underline{\phi}\): bad news
lowers value by more than good news of the same size raises it. (iii) At
given \((\lambda, K, X)\), \(V'\), \(|V''|\) and the difference in (ii)
increase in \(\phi\), and so does the semi-elasticity of the revenue
value \(A_{\text{eff}} X\) in \(\lambda\).}

\emph{Proof.} \(A_{\text{eff}} = (\Pi_L + \lambda \Pi_H)/\Delta\) is a
ratio of two affine functions of \(\lambda\), and neither \(\delta K/r\)
nor \(C_D/r\) depends on \(\lambda\). The first derivative is the
quotient-rule expression of the proof of Proposition 2(ii) times \(X\);
differentiating it again gives \(V''\), since \(G\) does not depend on
\(\lambda\). With \(\Delta > 0\) both signs are those of \(G\) and do
not change with \(\lambda\), so there is no range of convexity.
\(G > 0\) is Equation~\ref{eq-faith-condition} at monopoly shares, which
reduces to \((\phi/(1-\phi))^\alpha > (r - \mu_H)/(r - \mu_L)\), that is
\(\phi > \underline{\phi}\), exactly as in the symmetric case of that
proof (the shares cancel in both). This proves (i). For (ii),
\(A_{\text{eff}}(\lambda) - A_{\text{eff}}(\lambda') = G(\lambda - \lambda')/[\Delta(\lambda)\Delta(\lambda')]\),
so the loss from bad news is \(X G h/[\Delta(\Delta - h)]\) and the gain
from good news \(X G h/[\Delta(\Delta + h)]\); their difference is the
stated expression. For (iii),
\(\partial G/\partial \phi = \alpha[\Pi_H(r - \mu_L)/\phi + \Pi_L/(1-\phi)] > 0\),
and with \(\rho = \Pi_H/\Pi_L\), which increases in \(\phi\), the
semi-elasticity is
\(\partial \ln A_{\text{eff}}/\partial \lambda = (\rho(r - \mu_L) - 1)/[(1 + \lambda\rho)\Delta]\),
whose derivative in \(\rho\) is \(1/(1 + \lambda\rho)^2 > 0\).
\(\square\)

The identities in Equation~\ref{eq-installed-lambda}, in (ii) and in
(iii) are checked symbolically
(\texttt{symbolic\_duopoly.installed\_value\_in\_lambda}, asserted in
the test suite). The Lean package covers
Equation~\ref{eq-installed-lambda}, part (i) with its converse cases,
the identity in (ii) and its sign, and, for (iii),
\(\partial G/\partial \phi > 0\) and the monotonicity of the
semi-elasticity in \(\phi\), taking the going-concern value as given
(the scope paragraph at the start of this appendix).

Part (iii) holds at a common belief. It does not extend to firms whose
allocations differ because their beliefs differ: at the model's
allocation \(\phi^*(\lambda)\) the semi-elasticity of \(A_{\text{eff}}\)
in \(\lambda\) is \(2.24\) at \(\lambda = 0.05\), \(2.25\) at \(0.10\),
\(1.18\) at \(0.20\) and \(0.30\) at \(0.50\), because the second term
of Equation~\ref{eq-a-eff} saturates as \(\lambda\) grows.

\emph{What the corollary does not cover.} The installed firm of the
model of record also holds the option to stop: the shutdown option
without debt, the default option with it, with the coupled
regime-switching boundary of Section~\ref{sec-duopoly}. Its value in
\(\lambda\) has no closed form, and I check its shape numerically, on
\(33\) arrival rates spanning \([0.02, 0.80]\) at the baseline values of
the other parameters.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{Unlevered value with the shutdown option.} At \(K = 0.0158\) and
  \(\phi \in \{0.25, 0.30, 0.50, 0.70, 0.90, 0.99\}\), value is
  increasing and concave in \(\lambda\) at twelve demand levels from
  \(1.25\) to \(20\) times the highest shutdown boundary over that
  range, and at the single firm's precommitted-scale policy
  \((X^*, K^*, \phi^*)\), where the option adds less than \(1\%\) to the
  going-concern value. It fails near the boundary, where the option to
  stop is convex in \(\lambda\): at \(1.01\) times the boundary and
  \(\phi = 0.70\), value is increasing but not concave. Just above
  \(\underline{\phi}\) the option also overturns the sign close to the
  boundary: at \(\phi = 0.19\) value falls in \(\lambda\) at \(1.5\)
  times the boundary, although the going-concern value rises, and it is
  increasing and concave at three times the boundary.
\item
  \emph{Levered equity.} The equity of the installed firm depends on the
  coupon only through the total fixed cost \(q = \delta K + C_D\): the
  equity equations and the smooth-pasting condition are homogeneous of
  degree one in \((X, q)\), and recovery does not enter equity. Levered
  equity at demand \(X\) is therefore \(q/(\delta K)\) times unlevered
  value at demand \(X\,\delta K/q\), and has the same shape in
  \(\lambda\) at the same distance to its boundary. At the single firm's
  policy it is increasing and concave in \(\lambda\) under par debt at
  \(\ell = 0.40\) and \(0.70\).
\item
  \emph{The duopoly leader.} Before the follower enters, the leader's
  value also carries the dilution of its post-switch revenue by the
  follower, which comes sooner when \(\lambda\) is higher, so it is not
  \(A_{\text{eff}} X\) less a constant. At the leader's baseline policy
  \((K_L, \phi_L) = (0.0042, 0.679)\), with the follower re-solved at
  each \(\lambda\), its value is increasing and concave in \(\lambda\)
  at \(0.5\), \(1\) and \(1.5\) times \(X_L\), without debt and under
  par debt at \(\ell = 0.40\). At the same scale and \(\phi = 0.25\) the
  dilution term makes it non-monotone in \(\lambda\) on
  \([0.10, 0.50]\), although \(\phi > \underline{\phi}\).
\end{itemize}

These checks are pinned in
\texttt{tests/test\_valuation.py::TestValueInLambda}, which also keeps
the option-value checks of Figure~\ref{fig-lambda-option-value} apart
from them.

\subsubsection*{Proof of Proposition 3}\label{proof-of-proposition-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Proof of Proposition 3}

\emph{Maintained assumptions.} Assumption 1(A1)--(A2) and the game of
Section~\ref{sec-duopoly} in the exact primitives; Internet Appendix B
states the claims, the numerical method and its checks. Throughout this
proof \(F\) denotes the follower's value, not the single-firm option
value of Proposition 1. Part (i) and the first statement of part (ii)
are analytical. Everything else is a numerical finding, and the
paragraphs below record what was computed, on what grid, and what was
not shown.

\emph{Part (i): the follower after the switch (analytical).} After the
switch the regime is absorbing and the leader's capacity is installed,
so no decision of the leader remains and the follower faces a standard
investment problem under a geometric Brownian motion with drift
\(\mu_H\). Inference capacity earns nothing after the switch, so any
\(\phi_F < 1\) is dominated and the follower trains with all its
capacity. Entering at demand \(X\) with capacity \(K\) then pays
\(a(K)X - \Theta(K)\), with
\(a(K) = A_H K^{2\alpha}/(K^\alpha + T_L^\alpha) = A_H K^\alpha s_F(K)\)
the revenue coefficient of Equation~\ref{eq-contest-H} and
\(\Theta(K) = \delta K/r + I(K)\). For a given \(K\) this is the problem
of Step 1 of the Proof of Proposition 1: the trigger is
\([\beta_H/(\beta_H-1)]\,\Theta(K)/a(K)\) and the option coefficient is
proportional to \(a(K)^{\beta_H}/\Theta(K)^{\beta_H - 1}\). Its
logarithmic derivative with respect to \(\ln K\) is \[
\beta_H\,\alpha\,\bigl(2 - s_F(K)\bigr) - (\beta_H - 1)\,\frac{K\Theta'(K)}{\Theta(K)},
\] because \(\mathrm{d}\ln a/\mathrm{d}\ln K = \alpha(2 - s_F)\). The
share \(s_F\) is increasing in \(K\) and
\(K\Theta'/\Theta = (\delta K/r + \gamma c K^\gamma)/(\delta K/r + cK^\gamma)\)
rises from \(1\) to \(\gamma\), so the derivative is strictly decreasing
in \(K\). It is positive as \(K \to 0\) if and only if
\(2\alpha\beta_H > \beta_H - 1\) and negative as \(K \to \infty\) if and
only if \(\alpha\beta_H < \gamma(\beta_H - 1)\), which are the two
conditions of the statement. The coefficient therefore has a unique
interior maximizer, the root of Equation~\ref{eq-follower-foc}. With
\(T_L = 0\) the share is one and the condition is
Equation~\ref{eq-foc-K}, the single firm's post-switch problem of
Proposition 1(ii); the small-capacity condition is weaker than the upper
bound of (A2) because the follower's small-capacity elasticity is
\(2\alpha\).

Three qualifications. First, the statement is for the claim that
operates forever. With the option to stop, the entry payoff gains a term
proportional to \(X^{\beta_H^-}\) and the trigger and capacity are
computed numerically; at the baseline equilibrium they are \(0.18\%\)
and \(0.16\%\) lower. Second, the statement concerns entry at the
trigger. A follower that finds demand above \(X_F^*\) at the switch
enters at once, at the capacity that maximizes the entry payoff at that
demand; that the exercise region is the upper set \([X_F^*, \infty)\) is
checked with a linear complementarity problem and not proved. Third, the
comparison with the single firm in the statement (a larger and later
entrant) is a property of the solved policies at the baseline
calibration.

\emph{Part (ii): allocation (analytical critical point; equilibrium
allocation numerical).} Consider a firm that enters before the switch
against an installed rival. Once both firms are installed no decision
remains, so its installed value is \(A_{\text{eff},i}X - \delta K_i/r\)
with \(A_{\text{eff},i}\) given by Equation~\ref{eq-a-eff-duopoly},
exactly, for the claim that operates forever. Write the Tullock contest
payoff over the regime-relevant capacity measure \(u\) (with rival
measure \(\bar{u}\) fixed) as
\(f(u) = u^{2\alpha} / (u^\alpha + \bar{u}^\alpha)\). Differentiating,
\[
f'(u) = \alpha\, u^{\alpha - 1}\, s\,(2 - s), \qquad s = \frac{u^\alpha}{u^\alpha + \bar{u}^\alpha}:
\] the marginal contest revenue is the standalone marginal revenue
\(\alpha u^{\alpha-1}\) scaled by the common multiplier \(s(2-s)\). When
the two firms have the same training fraction \(\phi\), the
regime-relevant capacity ratios coincide across regimes
(\(u/\bar{u} = K_i/K_j\) for inference and training alike), so the share
\(s\), and hence the multiplier, is the same in the L-regime and
H-regime terms of the allocation condition: \[
\frac{\partial A_{\text{eff},i}}{\partial \phi_i}\bigg|_{\phi_i = \phi_j = \phi} = \alpha K_i^\alpha\, s\,(2 - s)\,\bigl[-w_L (1-\phi)^{\alpha-1} + w_H \phi^{\alpha-1}\bigr].
\] The multiplier cancels, leaving the single-firm condition of
Proposition 1, Step 5, whose unique zero is \(\phi^*\). Hence, for any
capacity pair, a common allocation \(\phi\) is a critical point of the
entrant's allocation problem if and only if \(\phi = \phi^*\): against a
rival at \(\phi^*\) the entrant's allocation condition is the single
firm's. That the critical point is a maximum, and the allocation against
a rival away from \(\phi^*\), are not characterized analytically.

In the equilibrium the premise of this statement does not hold, for two
reasons that the solved game makes visible. The follower enters after
the switch and trains with all its capacity (part i), so the leader does
not face a rival at its own allocation. And the leader's marginal
revenues are not those of Equation~\ref{eq-a-eff-duopoly}: its inference
revenue is a monopoly until the follower enters, while its training
revenue is shared once the follower has entered, so the marginal value
of training relative to inference is lower than the single firm's. The
leader's allocation is accordingly below \(\phi^*\) at every arrival
rate on the grid (Table~\ref{tbl-duopoly-beliefs}): by \(3.1\%\) at
baseline, by \(14\%\) at \(\lambda = 0.03\) and by \(0.2\%\) at
\(\lambda = 0.50\). The sign is argued, not proved; the magnitudes are
computed.

\emph{Part (iii): the equilibrium in which one firm leads at \(X_L\)
(numerical finding).} \emph{Strategies.} One firm is designated the
leader and the other the follower. The leader invests the first time
demand is at or above \(X_L\) before the switch, with the policy
\((K_L, \phi_L)\) that maximizes its entry value at that demand. The
follower does not invest before the leader has; once the leader is
installed it plays its best response (Internet Appendix B): it enters
before the switch where that is worth more than waiting, and otherwise
after the switch, at \(X_F^*\) or at once if the switch finds demand
above \(X_F^*\). If the switch arrives while neither firm is installed,
the two play the entry game of the absorbing regime, in which each is
worth the follower's value, and \(W(X)\) is the value before the switch
of the claim on that outcome. If the designated follower invests first,
the other firm responds as a follower.

\emph{Values before the first investment.} Until the leader invests
nothing is paid, so for \(X < X_L\) both values solve the L-regime
equation with \(\lambda\) times the post-switch value as forcing. They
are therefore \(W\) plus a multiple of \(X^{\beta_L^+}\), pinned by the
payoffs at \(X_L\):
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-values-before-entry}{
V_{\text{lead}}(X) = W(X) + \bigl[L^*(X_L) - W(X_L)\bigr]\Bigl(\frac{X}{X_L}\Bigr)^{\beta_L^+}, \qquad V_{\text{follow}}(X) = W(X) + \bigl[F(X_L) - W(X_L)\bigr]\Bigl(\frac{X}{X_L}\Bigr)^{\beta_L^+},
}\end{equation} with \(F(X_L)\) the follower's value against the policy
the leader installs at \(X_L\). The follower's value before the leader's
entry is \(V_{\text{follow}}\), the value of waiting for that entry or
for the switch. It is not \(F(X)\), which is what the follower would be
worth if the leader invested at \(X\).

\emph{The leader's deviations.} Given that the follower never invests
first, the leader faces a stopping problem with payoff \(L^*(X)\) and
waiting value \(W\). Investing at a trigger \(\bar{X}\) is worth
\(W(X) + h(\bar{X})X^{\beta_L^+}\) below it, with
\(h(X) = [L^*(X) - W(X)]X^{-\beta_L^+}\), so among triggers the best is
the maximizer of \(h\), which is Equation~\ref{eq-first-investment}. I
compute \(h\) on a logarithmic scan of 26 demand levels from \(0.03\) to
\(10\) times \(X_P\) and refine its maximum by a bounded search. At each
arrival rate \(h\) is negative at the bottom of the scan, where leading
is worth less than mutual waiting, rises to a single peak at \(X_L\) and
falls beyond it. A best trigger need not be a best stopping rule, so I
also solve the leader's problem without restricting it: with
\(u = V - W\) and \(\mathcal{A}\) the operator of
Equation~\ref{eq-hjb-L}, the linear complementarity problem
\(\min\{-\mathcal{A}u,\; u - (L^* - W)\} = 0\) is solved by policy
iteration on \(4{,}001\) log-spaced demand levels over the scanned
range, with \(L^* - W\) interpolated from the scan and no assumption on
the shape of the investment region. In every cell the leader invests at
every scanned demand level above a boundary and at none below it, the
boundary agrees with \(X_L\) to within \(0.14\%\), and the value below
it agrees with Equation~\ref{eq-values-before-entry} to within
\(3 \times 10^{-4}\) in relative terms.

\emph{The follower's deviations.} Investing first at \(X < X_L\) pays
the designated follower at most \(L^*(X)\), because the other firm then
follows. From Equation~\ref{eq-values-before-entry},
\(V_{\text{follow}}(X) - V_{\text{lead}}(X) = [F(X_L) - L^*(X_L)](X/X_L)^{\beta_L^+}\),
and \(V_{\text{lead}}(X) \geq L^*(X)\) for \(X \leq X_L\) is the
leader's condition just verified. The follower's condition therefore
holds at every demand level up to \(X_L\) whenever
\(F(X_L) \geq L^*(X_L)\), that is, whenever \(X_L \leq X_P\). On the
grid \(F/L^*\) at \(X_L\) is \(1.104\) at baseline and lies between
\(1.004\), at \(\lambda = 0.50\), and \(1.140\), at \(\lambda = 0.03\)
(Table~\ref{tbl-duopoly-order}). Evaluated directly at the scan points,
the follower's value of waiting exceeds the value of investing first by
at least \(6.8\%\) of the former at baseline and by at least \(0.3\%\)
in every cell. After the leader has invested, the follower's policy
solves its own stopping problem, which is checked against a linear
complementarity solve (Internet Appendix B). Off the equilibrium path,
if the leader has not invested at a demand level between \(X_L\) and
\(X_P\), the follower still prefers that the leader invest, because
\(L^* < F\) below \(X_P\). Above \(X_P\) both firms want to lead and the
strategies are not an equilibrium of the subgames that start there; when
the game starts below \(X_L\), demand cannot reach them before the
leader has invested.

\emph{What is and is not established.} The strategies are mutual best
responses, on the scan, in each of the \(45\) cells solved: the ten
arrival rates of the statement at zero leverage; the baseline arrival
rate at \(\ell = 0.20\) and the eight arrival rates from \(0.05\) to
\(0.50\) at \(\ell = 0.40\) and \(\ell = 0.65\), each under both debt
conventions; and the baseline with the follower's post-switch scale
precommitted. For the two fairly priced cells at \(\lambda = 0.05\) the
scan is narrower, seven demand levels from \(0.2\) to \(1.6\) times
\(X_P\), and the conditions are verified on that range only. The
conditions are checked at scan points and, between them, on an
interpolant, over a bounded range of demand; they are not proved. Four
further things are not established. First, uniqueness: I do not show
that no other asymmetric equilibrium exists. Second, the symmetric
equilibrium. Between \(X_L\) and \(X_P\) each firm prefers investing to
waiting for \(X_P\) and prefers that its rival invest, so a symmetric
equilibrium would involve randomized delay there, as in a war of
attrition; it is not solved, and where it would place the first
investment is not known beyond the bound \(X_P\). Third, coordination:
the model does not say which firm leads. Fourth, the continuation after
a switch that finds neither firm installed is a selection. \(W\) takes
the symmetric outcome of the post-switch race, in which each firm
expects the follower's value. If instead the firm that leads before the
switch were also the one to lead after it, its waiting value would be
higher, and at each arrival rate from \(0.075\) to \(0.50\) it would not
invest before the switch at any scanned demand level (at
\(\lambda = 0.05\) it would do so only at about ten times \(X_L\), above
\(X_P\); at \(\lambda = 0.03\), where \(X_L\) lies far below the
post-switch rent-equalization trigger and the two waiting values nearly
coincide, at the same \(X_L\)). Its rival, whose waiting value is \(W\),
would then be the firm that invests before the switch, at the same
\(X_L\), and the firm assured of leading after the switch would not gain
by investing ahead of it (on the scan its value of waiting exceeds the
value of investing first by at least \(0.3\%\) in every cell). The date
\(X_L\) thus requires only that the firm that invests before the switch
not be assured of leading after it. Simultaneous investment is not
priced, and leader policies that would make the follower enter at once
are excluded.

\emph{Part (iii), continued: the rent-equalization trigger \(X_P\)
(numerical finding).} The leader's best entry value
\(L^*(X) = \max_{K,\phi} L(X; K, \phi)\) and the follower's value
against the maximizing policy are computed on a logarithmic grid of 25
demand levels spanning four decades, from \(0.03\) to \(300\) times the
single firm's post-switch trigger \(X_H^*\), and the first change of
sign of \(L^* - F\) from negative to positive is refined by Brent's
method (Internet Appendix B). A trigger is found at each of the ten
arrival rates of the statement at zero leverage, and at each of the
eight arrival rates from \(0.05\) to \(0.50\) at \(\ell = 0.40\) and
\(\ell = 0.65\) under both debt conventions (on a narrower scan for the
two fairly priced cells at \(\lambda = 0.05\);
Table~\ref{tbl-spread-entry}). In every cell solved the gap changes sign
once on the scan, and \(X_L < X_P\).

The existence argument available for the nested pure-power game does not
carry over, and I have no analytical substitute. There the leader's
scale is fixed, so the sunk outlay makes the leader's entry value
strictly negative at the origin while the follower's value vanishes, and
an intermediate-value argument applies once the sign at an upper
endpoint is checked. Here the leader may choose a vanishing scale, so
both \(L^*\) and \(F\) tend to zero at the origin and the sign of their
difference near it is itself a computed fact, as is the sign above the
trigger. Uniqueness is not claimed. With the leader's policy held fixed
the gap crosses zero upward and then downward again, as in the standard
preemption literature; with the leader's policy chosen at each demand
level the scan shows one crossing at the baseline value of \(\gamma\),
and the diagnostic computation of this game without the option to stop
found more than one at \(\gamma = 1.9\) for \(\lambda \geq 0.10\). The
trigger reported is the first up-crossing.

Three further facts bear on the role of \(X_P\). First, it is an upper
bound on the date of first investment and not that date: above it each
firm would rather lead than follow, so no equilibrium has both firms
waiting there, while at \(X_L\), below it, a firm that knows its rival
will follow already prefers to invest. Second, of the two conditions
under which \citet{huisman2015strategic} show the sequential outcome to
be the equilibrium of the preemption game, one holds at every cell: the
follower's entry, before or after the switch, lies above \(X_P\), and at
baseline the follower never enters before the switch. The other, that
leading is worth more than investing simultaneously with the rival, is
not checked in the exact game, in which simultaneous installation is not
priced. Third, the result depends on the leader's scale being a best
response: with the leader held at the pure-power policy the gap is
negative at every demand level at baseline, and with the leader held at
the single firm's precommitted-scale policy a trigger exists at
\(0.0077\), where leading is worth \(0.94\) of mutual waiting (Internet
Appendix B).

\emph{Parts (iv) and (v) and Remark 4 (numerical findings).} These have
no analytical derivation. The shapes in \(\lambda\) of the trigger, the
leader's capacity, the distance to the boundary at entry and the spread
at entry are read off the solved equilibria in
Table~\ref{tbl-duopoly-beliefs} and Table~\ref{tbl-spread-entry}, and
the effects of leverage under the two debt conventions off
Table~\ref{tbl-duopoly-leverage}. The mechanism behind the spread in
part (v) is that two things move with \(\lambda\) besides the distance
\(X_L/X_D\): the root \(\beta_L^-\), which becomes more negative as
\(\lambda\) rises and so lowers the value of the default claim at a
given distance, and the level of the boundary relative to the coupon.
The earlier statement that the spread at entry is weakly increasing in
\(\lambda\) was not backed by a computation and does not hold.
\(\square\)

\subsubsection*{Numerical Finding 1 (Dario's
dilemma)}\label{numerical-finding-1-darios-dilemma}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Numerical Finding 1 (Dario's
dilemma)}

Define the policy-evaluation function
\(W(\lambda_{\text{invest}}) = \text{NPV}(\lambda_{\text{true}}, \lambda_{\text{invest}})\):
the present value when the firm invests at trigger
\(X^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}})\) with capacity
\(K^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}})\) and training fraction
\(\phi^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}})\), but demand outcomes are realized
under the true process governed by \(\lambda_{\text{true}}\). The
operating policy \((X^*, K^*, \phi^*)\) is always the solution to the
\emph{unlevered} problem at belief \(\lambda_{\text{invest}}\): the firm
sizes and times its investment on NPV grounds, and exogenous leverage
\(\ell\) is layered on afterwards. For \(\ell = 0\), \(W\) is the
unlevered NPV of that policy; for \(\ell > 0\), \(W\) is the value of
the levered claims net of the outlay, \(E + D - I(K^*)\) with \(E\) the
going-concern equity, evaluated under the same
belief-\(\lambda_{\text{invest}}\) operating policy.

\emph{Optimality.} At \(\ell = 0\) the policy
\((X^*, K^*, \phi^*)(\lambda_{\text{invest}})\) maximizes precisely the
objective that \(W\) evaluates, so \(W\) is maximized at
\(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = \lambda_{\text{true}}\) and
\(W'(\lambda_{\text{true}}) = 0\) by construction. The second-order loss
is therefore
\(\Delta V \approx -\frac{1}{2} W''(\lambda_{\text{true}})(\lambda_{\text{invest}} - \lambda_{\text{true}})^2\),
which is symmetric to second order.

For \(\ell > 0\) this step must be weakened, because the operating
policy optimizes the unlevered NPV while \(W\) values the levered
claims: the belief that maximizes the levered \(W\) is not exactly
\(\lambda_{\text{true}}\), and \(W'(\lambda_{\text{true}}) = 0\) holds
only as an approximation. The discrepancy is numerically negligible. At
the baseline calibration with \(\ell = 0.40\), the levered \(W\) peaks
at \(\lambda_{\text{invest}} \approx 0.101\) rather than at
\(\lambda_{\text{true}} = 0.10\) (the levered objective tilts marginally
toward optimism, because the shareholders' default option is worth more
to a firm that enters closer to its default boundary), and
\(W(\lambda_{\text{true}})\) falls short of that maximum by about
\(1.3 \times 10^{-5}\) of its value, roughly one thousandth of one
percent, against the 5--26\% belief-mismatch losses the exercise
reports. The expansion below is therefore exact for \(\ell = 0\) and is
applied to \(\ell > 0\) as an approximation whose error is more than
three orders of magnitude smaller than the effects it describes.

\emph{Source of asymmetry.} The asymmetry arises from odd-order terms in
the Taylor expansion, principally
\(W'''(\lambda_{\text{true}}) \neq 0\). The following heuristic argument
identifies three contributing channels. The sign of \(W'''\) is verified
computationally by central finite differences at a fixed reference
demand \(X_0 = X^*(\lambda_{\text{true}})/2\), with two step sizes
(\(0.1\lambda_{\text{true}}\) and \(0.2\lambda_{\text{true}}\)) that
must agree; the loss ranking is verified at the matched pair
\(\lambda_{\text{true}}(1 \pm 0.8)\) and at the exhibit pair
\((0.2, 2.0)\lambda_{\text{true}}\). Both checks are run at the baseline
calibration and at each admissible draw of the \(\pm 25\%\)
one-at-a-time parameter sweep reported in Internet Appendix E; that
sweep, and not a search over the joint parameter space, is what the
robustness of these signs rests on. The expansion is local: a positive
third derivative orders the losses for small equal errors,
\(\Delta V(-h) - \Delta V(+h) = \tfrac{1}{3}W'''(\lambda_{\text{true}})h^3 + O(h^5)\),
and at the baseline that term accounts for the whole gap at \(h = 0.02\)
(\(0.65\%\) against \(0.43\%\)) but not at \(h = 0.08\), where the
losses are \(26\%\) and \(4\%\); the finite-error ranking is what the
matched-pair check establishes.

\emph{Case \(\ell = 0\) (unlevered).} The loss decomposes into a timing
channel and a \emph{training allocation channel}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \emph{Capacity channel.} Since \(K^*\) is independent of both \(\phi\)
  and \(\lambda\) under the pure-power approximation
  (Equation~\ref{eq-trigger-phi}), belief mismatches do not distort
  capacity choice. The capacity channel contributes zero asymmetry.
\item
  \emph{Timing channel.} The timing factor \((X_0/X^*)^{\beta}\) is
  approximately symmetric, contributing only weakly to the asymmetry.
\item
  \emph{Training allocation channel (dominant).} A pessimistic firm sets
  \(\phi^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}}) \ll \phi^*(\lambda_{\text{true}})\),
  drastically reducing the H-regime component of \(A_{\text{eff}}\). At
  baseline, the H-regime term accounts for approximately 70\% of
  \(A_{\text{eff}}\), so under-training destroys the dominant source of
  firm value. By contrast, an optimistic firm sets
  \(\phi^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}}) > \phi^*(\lambda_{\text{true}})\),
  sacrificing L-regime inference revenue but retaining the H-regime
  upside. The asymmetry in \(W(\lambda_{\text{invest}})\) arises from
  the composition of two effects: (i) the dominance of the H-regime
  component in \(A_{\text{eff}}\) at baseline, which means
  under-training destroys more value than over-training sacrifices, and
  (ii) the nonlinear mapping \(\lambda \mapsto \phi^*(\lambda)\), whose
  elasticity \(\varepsilon_{\phi^*,\lambda} = (1-\phi^*)/(1-\alpha)\) is
  decreasing in \(\lambda\) and is \emph{large} precisely on the
  pessimistic side: at \(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = 0.02\), where
  \(\phi^* = 0.14\), the elasticity is \(\varepsilon \approx 1.44\),
  against \(\varepsilon \approx 0.50\) at the baseline
  \(\lambda_{\text{true}} = 0.10\) and \(\varepsilon \approx 0.05\) at
  \(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = 0.50\). A proportional belief error of a
  given size therefore distorts the allocation far more below
  \(\lambda_{\text{true}}\) than above it, amplifying policy distortions
  on the pessimistic side.
\end{itemize}

The training allocation channel dominates, so the computed
\(W'''(\lambda_{\text{true}})\) is positive at the baseline calibration
(\(1.17\) and \(1.28\) per unit of \(X_0^{\beta_H}\) at the two step
sizes) and at every admissible draw of the sweep, and underinvestment
(\(\lambda_{\text{invest}} < \lambda_{\text{true}}\)) produces a larger
expected-value loss than overinvestment of equal magnitude, at the
matched pair in every draw.

\emph{Case \(\ell > 0\) (levered).} Leverage operates through the
default channel. Because \(K^*\) is belief-independent, the coupon
\(r_c \ell I(K^*)\) is identical across belief types, so the channel
runs through the allocation and the trigger alone: over-training
(\(\phi^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}}) \gg \phi^*(\lambda_{\text{true}})\))
lowers L-regime inference revenue and pushes the default boundary
\(X_D\) up, while the lower entry trigger
\(X^*(\lambda_{\text{invest}})\) simultaneously moves entry down toward
that boundary. The two effects compound and compress the distance to
default at entry, whereas underinvestment moves the trigger \emph{away}
from the boundary, largely offsetting the allocation distortion.

In the total-claim metric \(E + D - I\) this compression registers as a
small \emph{gain}, not a loss: it raises the value of the shareholders'
default option, which is the only term separating the levered from the
unlevered objective and which is worth \(1.8\%\) of NPV to the
overinvesting firm against \(0.3\%\) at correct beliefs. Both levered
loss curves therefore lie marginally below their unlevered counterparts
(at baseline, \(26.1\%\) and \(21.4\%\) at
\(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = 0.02\) and \(0.50\), against \(26.2\%\) and
\(22.6\%\) at \(\ell = 0\)), and what leverage amplifies is the
\emph{ratio} of the two losses (from \(1.16\) to \(1.22\)), not their
levels. The economically substantive effect of leverage is on the tail
rather than the mean, and it runs in the \emph{opposite direction} to
the expected-value asymmetry: the five-year default probability under
overinvestment (\(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = 0.50\), \(\ell = 0.40\)) is
nearly eight times the baseline, while underinvestment barely raises
default risk, because the conservative firm enters at a higher (safer)
trigger.

\textbf{Levered wedge and default-probability asymmetry.} The wedge
between the levered and unlevered loss curves is exactly the
shareholders' default option,
\([R(X_D) + \delta K/r - A_{\text{eff}} X_D](X^*/X_D)^{\beta_L^-}\). At
this calibration the recovery cap binds (\(R(X_D) = C_D/r\), so
creditors are made whole in value terms), leaving the bracket equal to
the flow deficit the firm escapes at default, which is overwhelmingly
the perpetual operating-cost drain: \(\delta K/r\) is about eighteen
times the coupon claim \(C_D/r\) at baseline. Because \(K^*\), and hence
both the coupon and \(\delta K\), is belief-invariant, and the boundary
condition pins \(A_{\text{eff}} X_D\) to the same constant for every
belief type, the bracket itself is identical across the conservative,
correctly calibrated, and aggressive firms; only the discount factor
\((X^*/X_D)^{\beta_L^-}\) differs, and it is this factor alone that
produces the differential option value reported above. The
default-probability comparison, evaluated at each firm's own optimal
policy rather than at the fixed \(X = 0.10\), \(K = 1\), \(\phi = 0.5\)
used for the credit-spread grid of Internet Appendix H, makes the
eight-times figure concrete. A conservative firm
(\(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = 0.02\)) faces a five-year default
probability of \(0.79\%\) at \(\ell = 0.40\), only slightly above the
\(0.64\%\) baseline, because it enters at a higher trigger
(\(X^* = 0.0055\) against \(0.0047\) at correct beliefs), and the later
entry nearly offsets the adverse effect of its own allocation distortion
on the default boundary. An aggressive firm
(\(\lambda_{\text{invest}} = 0.50\)) faces \(5.04\%\), because it enters
at a much lower trigger (\(X^* = 0.0033\)) while over-training
(\(\phi^* = 0.97\) against \(0.70\)) depresses L-regime inference
revenue and pushes the default boundary up; its distance to default at
entry, \(X^*/X_D\), falls from \(5.1\) to \(3.3\), whereas the
conservative firm's falls only to \(4.9\). Capacity plays no part in
this asymmetry: \(K^*\) is independent of \(\lambda\) (Proposition 1),
so all three firms install identical capacity and carry identical coupon
obligations, and the entire effect runs through the entry trigger and
the training allocation.
